A camera that disconnects loses its tab and lease; its zoom, pan, display settings, and tool are kept in memory and given back when it reconnects, and the tool of each camera tab is also given back after a reload of the tab (`cameraToolsEntry` of `web/src/profile/view.ts`), as is the camera tab selected last (`activeCameraTabEntry`, sessionStorage), once its camera has a tab again, unless the operator selected another camera tab first.
- A camera tab shows one frame at a time by its selection: `latest`, the camera's latest available frame (after a restart, the newest saved frame, labelled Latest saved), followed as each newer frame in memory arrives, only the newest of those arriving while one opens being shown; `saved`, a saved frame, which new frames never replace; `liveStack`, a snapshot of a stack or a color composition of the Live Stacking session of the camera (`showLiveStack`), asked for again when its stacks grow (`followLiveStacks`) and replaced by the latest frame when the session closes, while the frame under it keeps its lease (see Live Stacking Contract); or `inspect`, the frame held when Run Detection or Solve Image starts on a followed frame, or a star of it is inspected (`holdCameraFrame`), so the result stays on the frame analyzed. Return to Latest follows the camera again without replaying the frames in between, and a capture accepted from the Camera Acquisition panel of the tab does the same for an `inspect` frame (`releaseCameraFrame`), so the tab shows what the capture takes; a `saved` frame stays selected. A selected frame that cannot be opened keeps the frame shown with the error and never falls back to another frame.
- Showing a frame is one transaction (`showCameraFrame` in `web/src/images/cameratabs.ts`): the frame is leased and rendered with the tab's applied display settings (before any, those its camera saved in the profile, or the frame's defaults; settings being applied meanwhile become the applied ones and are saved), plus its original rendering during Show Original, while the previous frame stays shown; then the frame, its document, and its renderings are committed together, zoom and pan are adapted to its size (`adaptView` in `web/src/viewer/transform.ts`: same size kept, a new size keeps the relative centre and the fraction shown, Fit stays Fit), the selected star, the star of the Star Profile Inspector, and the solution are cleared, and the previous lease is released. A frame asked for later supersedes one still opening, whose lease and renderings are released. Settings being edited in a popup of the viewer toolbar are never applied by a frame change. Overlays and results follow the image id of the frame shown, and the Image Inspector shows its camera, connection, origin, frame, storage state, and why it is shown, with the session of an Auto Focus, TPPA, Mount Pointing, or DARV frame and what it was taken for: the measurement or verification and the focuser position its Auto Focus session recorded (`autoFocusFrameFocus`; nothing when not recorded yet), the TPPA step, or the DARV measurement and its result. A frame without a known star detection fetches the jobs kept for its image (`GET /api/images/:id/stars`), so the measurements of Auto Focus are shown without a new detection.
- Below the viewer of a camera tab, the capture frames carousel (`web/src/images/CaptureFramesCarousel.tsx`) shows the Latest card first (the latest available frame, Latest saved after a restart, marked New while another card is selected and a newer frame exists), then the saved frames newest first, without the frame Latest already shows. Cards show metadata only (number, time, exposure, a floppy disk icon once the frame's file is written, the Latest card included, a state only while loading, failed, or missing, and a tooltip with id, size, file, and availability; the selected card is marked by its outline alone) and an icon in the area reserved for a future thumbnail; no pixels are loaded for them, and the saved frames of a camera are fetched when its tab is shown, and an Older frames card at the end of the strip fetches the next page of older ones while the camera has some (`loadOlderFrames`); once one was fetched, the frames of the camera are kept whole, and before that a frame pushed out of the first page becomes the cursor of the older ones. A saved frame that cannot be opened shows why and cannot be selected. Arrow keys, Home, and End move between cards, Enter or Space selects one; selecting a card only opens and renders its frame, never an analysis. The toolbar hides and shows the carousel without changing the selection. A card and the viewer toolbar number a frame by `frame.sequence`, the order in the whole registry, so frames of different sessions never share a number; it grows with gaps, since revisions take from the same counter. `generation` counts within a session and reads "Frame of session".
- On every socket open the snapshot is fetched first (a camera an event of the same server run made newer is kept), then the leases of the camera tabs are reconciled and each tab shows its selection again: a lost lease reopens the same saved or held frame from its file, or the latest one; without a frame to show, the tab keeps its pixels and says the frame is no longer available.
- A fixed or loop LIGHT capture dithers between its frames when its request asks for it (`CaptureRequest.dither`, a frozen snapshot; absent, or on a single exposure or another frame type, it never dithers). The capture keeps the cadence: it dithers before the frame that follows each `everyFrames` frames taken (`ditherBoundary`), after the frame sink's gate and the inter-frame delay, so the delay is followed by the dither and its settle; never before the first frame, after the last, during an exposure, or after a stop or an abort. The dithering service (`CaptureDithering`, `api/src/dithering.ts`) prepares the method the request names when the capture is admitted, refusing it before anything starts, and runs each dither with a token of the capture and the frame, so a repeated boundary never dithers twice; there is no fallback to another method and no skipped dither: a failed dither ends the capture as failed with its reason, the frames already taken kept. The session publishes the `dithering` phase and `dither` (completed dithers, whether a mechanical settle runs, the error that ended it), and the capture ends, releasing what its dithers held, only once nothing they commanded still moves. **Guided**: the dithers are commands of the named native guiding session (`admitDither` through `GuidingSessions.claimDither`), which must be guiding when the capture starts; the claim keeps the manual Dither and any other claimant refused as busy for the whole capture, while the corrections go on; each dither resolves with the outcome of its exact command once the guider settled (amount, RA only, and settle of the guide camera's Guiding form). Nothing physical is held for them, so a stop or an abort ends the capture at once while the guider finishes its own settle. A Target Centering hold on the guiding session's connection fails the capture at its next dither. **Mount**: the toolkit's `MountDitherer`, one per capture so its pattern (random, spiral, golden, or grid) runs across it, moves an unguided mount by an angle on the sky (arcseconds in the request) with one timed pulse per axis through the guide output the request names (the mount's own port, or a camera's ST4 port the operator vouches drives that mount; the guide rates of that output and the guide directions of a positive RA and DEC offset, East and North by the nominal convention of the mount's commands unless the operator reversed one for that mount, turn the angle into pulses, and a pulse longer than the request's limit fails without moving; no calibration measures those directions, so a reversed one moves the mount the other way, the offsets are then the intended ones and not the physical motion, and Spiral and Grid come out mirrored, which the panel warns about), or with a small exact GoTo of a tracking, unparked mount (never corrected by a pointing model) that cannot flip it, then waits a fixed mechanical settle, which measures nothing. The capture is refused while guiding runs on the connection of the mount or the guide output (`409 guidingActive`), when the guide output is its own camera, or while another operation holds either; otherwise the mount and the guide output are reserved for the whole capture (or must already be reserved by the caller's invocation, which is then reused), so every manual command of them is refused as busy until the capture ends, and guiding is held off their connections. Each dither runs as a root of that reservation beside the capture; a stop or an abort cancels it, and the end of the capture waits for the motion to drain and for the observed end of its pulses before the reservation is given back. A mount lost between two dithers fails the next one as `disconnected`. A GoTo dither never flips a German equatorial mount knowingly, from evidence and never the time alone: the capture is refused (`409 meridianFlip`), and a dither fails without moving (through the toolkit's `beforeSlew` on its exact target), while the Meridian Flip Monitor of the mount reports a flip candidate or while the pier side the geometry expects for the target (its hour angle at the mount's site by the host clock) differs from the one the mount reports; a mount other than altazimuth or fork that reports no pier side or no site is refused for GoTo dithers (`422 unsupported`), since a flip could not be ruled out. A GoTo after which the mount reports another pier side fails the capture, since the field turned. Pulses never flip a mount and are not checked. A dither near the celestial pole whose disc contains it fails without moving. Dither outcomes stay in the session and never enter the frame headers.
- Besides the lossy preview, `CameraCaptures.listenFrames(sink)` (`api/src/captures.ts`) feeds one `CaptureFrameSink` with every LIGHT frame of Camera Acquisition exactly once, identified by its `frameId`: `captured` as each frame is decoded, before the preview may drop it, with an owned copy of its bytes for Preview Only and none for Preview + Save, whose file follows with `saved` once its write completed or `saveFailed`, and `captureEnded` when the session ends. The sink is also asked through the `beforeFrame` gate of the toolkit capturer before each frame is prepared, so it can hold the next exposure back (never the one in flight); the gate settles on stop, abort, or failure of the capture and never ends it. Frames of other frame types and of the composite operations (Auto Focus, TPPA, DARV, the wizards, Target Centering, Mount Pointing) never reach it, nor guide frames. A sink never commands the camera, and its failures are logged and never fail the capture.
- Limitations: a previewed-only frame can be opened only while it is the latest of its camera or leased; a tab asleep for longer than the grace period may lose such frames; analysis results are keyed by image id, so a frame reopened from its file shows none of the results of its first document; Latest is the latest frame the preview decoded, and a loop may skip frames between two previews; only the cameras of the watched connection have tabs, so the frames of another connection's cameras are not shown; a disconnected camera has no tab, so its saved frames are reachable again only once it reconnects; the setup has no optical train, so the focuser and mount of a camera tab follow a fixed rule (the FOCUSER only for the MAIN_CAMERA, the MOUNT for every camera of the connection).

## Overlay Contracts

- A toggle of the viewer toolbar hides every overlay of a document at once and draws them again as they were (`ImageViewState.overlaysHidden`, tab memory, per document, kept across its frames), changing none of their settings. While hidden, a click selects nothing and the measure modes and the drawing actions of the context menu are unavailable, so nothing acts on what cannot be seen; the Guiding star pick stays, and so does the coordinate readout of the status line.
- Overlays draw through `ImageViewer`'s `overlays(size, view)` callback as SVG in **image pixel coordinates** (origin at the top-left of the first pixel). Zoom, pan, fit, 1:1, and resize are applied by the viewer, so overlays never compute screen coordinates; `view` gives the scale, for sizes constant on screen, and the visible region, to skip what is off screen. The viewer applies zoom and pan to the overlays as an SVG transform on a layer outside the CSS-transformed image, so strokes and dashes use `vectorEffect="non-scaling-stroke"` to keep their width on screen; a stroke inside a CSS transform would scale with it. Toolkit results use 0-based pixel indices with integer centres, so a result point (x, y) is drawn at (x + 0.5, y + 0.5).
- An overlay is a pure function of a result and the image shown: it draws only results whose image id matches the displayed image. Hiding an overlay never cancels or deletes the result, and showing one never re-runs the analysis.
- Celestial-coordinate overlays (annotation, grid) require a WCS solution of that same image: the one `usePlateSolution` selects (the chosen job or the header). Without one they are unavailable, not approximated. The WCS overlay (`web/src/platesolving/WcsOverlay.tsx`) draws the solved centre and north and east arrows from `web/src/platesolving/wcs.ts` (the toolkit's TAN/TAN-SIP transforms; each is the projection of a step along the great circle leaving the point towards that direction, so they stay true at a pole and beside it, where the direction of a step along a parallel would wrap around the pole, measured from the projection of the point itself, so an inverse SIP that does not close on the forward one by a few pixels, as ASTAP can write, does not turn them); the arrows and marker keep their screen size until they would exceed a quarter of the image's smaller side, then shrink with it. The RA/Dec grid (`WcsGridOverlay.tsx`, geometry in `grid.ts`) is shown per document independently of the centre: it covers the visible part of the image plus a 50 % margin, chooses its major step from a ladder of 1″ to 30° and 1s to 6h by the spacing of neighbouring lines measured across them on screen at five points (so rotation, SIP, and the convergence of meridians near a pole are followed), keeps a step while its spacing stays within 60–260 px, divides it into minor lines at least 18 px apart, traces each line with a subdivision bounded by a 0.5 px chord error (16 initial segments, depth 8, 256 segments), breaks it where the projection fails or jumps, and clips it to the region; at most 24 major lines per axis. The geometry is cached per solution while the view stays inside its region and within √2 of its zoom, so a pointer move never retraces it. Major lines are labelled where they cross the boundary of the visible image (declinations left then right, right ascensions bottom then top), horizontally at a constant 11 px, at most 48, dropping labels that would overlap on their side. Meridians and parallels are drawn in one theme color, or each in its own (reddish right ascension, bluish declination), lines and labels alike. The passive aids follow the same selected WCS without any request: the status line shows the right ascension, declination, frame, and local pixel scale under the pointer (`WcsCursorStatus`, one unprojection per move, "—" off the image or where the WCS fails); `WcsAidsOverlay` draws an optional crosshair, the compact north and east arrows 44 px inside the top-right corner of the visible image from the WCS at that point (an arrow that cannot be projected is left out; the orientation mode can draw them at the solved centre instead, or hide them), and the scale bar near the bottom-left corner: the longest round angle from 1″ to 5° spanning 60–180 px, its end found by bisection on the separation through the WCS where it is drawn, hidden when the two axis scales differ by more than 10 % there or it does not fit (`web/src/platesolving/aids.ts`). Coordinates are written in the profile's format and decimals (`coordinates.ts`), a display choice that claims no accuracy. Its `fitsToViewer` is the only place where a FITS pixel (1-based, integer centres) becomes a viewer point (minus half a pixel); the TPPA correction guide, whose geometry the server computes with the toolkit in that convention, converts through it too.
- `ImageViewer`'s `onImageClick(point, view)` reports a click (never a drag or a pinch) in image coordinates; the star markers use it to pick a star. The viewer stays unaware of what its consumers draw or measure; its other pointer contracts are generic and optional:
    - `overlays(size, view, cursor)` and `status(cursor)` receive the `ViewerCursor` (image point, zero-based pixel inside the image, viewport point), absent off the viewport, so the status line and overlays follow the pointer without re-rendering their owner.
    - `interaction` (`ViewerInteraction` of `web/src/viewer/interaction.ts`) lets a tool take presses: `onPointerDown` decides on the first pointer whether the tool owns the gesture, otherwise the viewer pans; hovers reach the tool too. The pure router `routeGesture` turns a second pointer into a pinch that cancels the tool (`onCancel`), as do `pointercancel` and a lost capture; nothing resumes until every pointer is lifted. A press of the pan released within the click tolerance is a click. Space held on the focused viewport pans whatever the tool; Escape cancels and Delete or Backspace deletes (`onDelete`) only while the viewport has the focus, so form controls keep their keys.
    - The pointer over the image is a crosshair, whatever the tool, since any point of it can be clicked, read, or drawn from; it is the hand of the pan only while the image is being dragged, past the click tolerance so a click never shows it, or while Space is held.
    - `onImageContextMenu(point, client, view)` answers the native `contextmenu` event (a right click, or a long press on touch devices) and returns whether it handled it; otherwise the browser's own menu opens.
    - No screen-space layer exists: an overlay keeps a size constant on screen with `1 / view.scale` and places corner symbols from `view.visible`.
- An ROI is a region in image pixels. It never changes a camera's physical subframe unless an explicit, capability-checked command does it.

## World Map Contract

- `WorldMap` (`web/src/ui/WorldMap.tsx`) draws the basemap `web/assets/world.map.svg` (equirectangular, 2520.631 × 1260.315 map units, longitude −180° at x = 0 and latitude +90° at y = 0) with zoom (wheel, pinch, double click, `+`/`-`, buttons, from the whole map up to `MAX_WORLD_MAP_ZOOM` = 64×) and pan (drag, arrows), and reports a click, never a drag or a pinch, as a geographic position through `onSelect`; the owner keeps the selection and passes it back as `selected`, drawn as a marker. Geographic positions are degrees, longitude east positive. The owner moves the view through the `WorldMapHandle` ref (`centerOn`, `zoomBy`, `reset`).
- The map wraps across the antimeridian by default: the basemap and the children are drawn again one map width west and east, panning east or west never ends, and the smallest zoom fits the map's width. The graticule then draws the antimeridian once, at x = 0, as the seam between copies. With `wrap={false}` the whole map fits, the view is clamped to it, and the antimeridian is only its border.
- Children are SVG in map units, drawn over the basemap and the graticule: `geoPath` (unwrapped across ±180° by default, or split for a map that does not wrap) and `horizonRegionPath` (where a body is above or below the horizon, from its subpoint: the night side, the visibility of a lunar eclipse), and `smallCircleRegionPath` (a spherical cap around a point, such as a satellite's footprint, closed along the map's edge when it holds a pole) in `web/src/ui/worldmap.geometry.ts` build their path data; `splitAtAntimeridian` cuts a line of several turns around the globe (a ground track) into pieces that meet at ±180°, so none is unwrapped over several map widths, and `WorldMapSymbol` places symbols of constant screen size. Toolkit eclipse paths fit when projected into the same map units; the API draws them on the degree plane (x = longitude + 180, y = 90 − latitude, in degrees), which `DEGREE_PLANE_TRANSFORM` scales to map units. Strokes use `vectorEffect="non-scaling-stroke"`.
- Zoom and pan never re-render React: the view is written to the layers once per animation frame. The basemap is a tile pyramid on a `<canvas>` (`web/src/ui/worldmap.tiles.ts`): level L is the whole map 2^L tiles of 256 device pixels across, up to level 11, rasterized from the SVG's shapes by a worker (`web/src/ui/worldmap.worker.ts`, started from a Blob of self-contained functions, so it needs OffscreenCanvas 2D in workers), so a frame only draws bitmaps already made and never waits for the thousands of country paths. A missing tile is drawn from the matching part of the nearest coarser cached tile and fades in when it arrives; levels 0 to 3 are fetched first and never evicted, so the map is never blank, and at most 256 tiles are kept. Borders keep the SVG's width up to 1.5 device pixels; the children are on an SVG layer with the same zoom and pan, which also sets `--world-map-scale` (viewport pixels per map unit). Children should be memoized by their owner, since the map renders them again whenever its own props change.

## Observing Sites and Time Zones Contract

| Concept                         | Owner                                                           | Meaning                                                                                                                                          |
| ------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Observing site                  | `api`, database (`observing_sites`), mirrored in `siteState`    | A named place: geodetic latitude and longitude (degrees, east positive), height above the WGS84 ellipsoid (meters), and its IANA civil time zone |
| Active site                     | `Profile.activeSiteId` (`profiles.active_site_id`)              | The site a profile observes from; none is a valid state, and then nothing assumes a place                                                        |
| Display zone                    | `ProfileSettings.dateTime` (Settings → Date & Time)             | How civil times read in every screen of the profile: the site's zone, UTC, or the browser's                                                      |
| Tool location and zone override | tab memory of each tool (`ToolLocation`, `DisplayZoneOverride`) | A place and a zone chosen for one tool alone: another saved site or coordinates typed in with their zone; never written to the profile or a site |
| Instants                        | every request and result                                        | Unix milliseconds (UTC), or the toolkit's scales; never changed by a zone                                                                        |
| Mount site                      | the mount driver                                                | The site the mount reports; it equals a site only once Send Active Site to Mount wrote it                                                        |

- The catalog is one per server, shared by every profile, tab, and browser, and edited in Settings → Observing Sites (`web/src/settings/ObservingSitesSettings.tsx`, `ObservingSiteEditor.tsx`): a name, coordinates typed as decimal or sexagesimal degrees with a sign or a hemisphere letter (`parseGeographicAngle`) and kept in decimal degrees, the ellipsoidal height, and a zone always chosen by a person (the browser's is only offered); the world map sets the coordinates and never the zone. Names and coordinates may repeat; ids are generated by the tab. The top bar's site menu selects the active site in one click. Selecting, editing, or deleting a site changes the workspace only: no device is commanded, no operation restarted, and a running operation keeps the place it started with (operations receive coordinates by value, or read the mount's own site).
- `web/src/location/` derives the rest, storing nothing: `activeSite`, `resolveToolLocation` (the active site followed live, a saved site, or typed coordinates whose zone is UTC until chosen), `siteObserver` (the `{ latitude, longitude, elevation }` the API takes), and `locationKey` (geographic only, so a renamed site or a new zone reloads nothing that depends on the place alone). `ToolLocationSection` is the Location section every tool with a place of its own uses, and `TimeZoneSelect` the zone override every tool that offers one uses.
- One rule shows a civil time (`resolveDisplayZone` of `web/src/location/zone.ts`): a tool's override when it made one (an IANA zone, UTC, the browser's, or the site of the tool), otherwise the profile's display zone; the site mode takes the tool's own location (a saved site other than the active one, or typed coordinates), and without one the browser's zone stands in, named as such. Zones are always IANA identifiers, never offsets or an empty sentinel; offsets are shown for an instant only (`formatUtcOffset`).
- A local day or night (`localDay` of the Sun almanac, `/api/astro-weather/night`) is always named in the zone of the place observed, whatever zone the times are shown in. LMST, LAST, hour angles, rise, transit, set, passes, and visibility depend on the place and the instant only; UTC, Julian dates, and timescales keep their own definitions.
- Wall times typed in a zone (`wallTimeToInstant`) name one instant, two when the clocks repeat them, or none when they skip them: the Sky time bar shows the first of two and refuses a skipped one, each saying so; the Astronomical Time Converter keeps its Earlier and Later choice and refuses a skipped reading through its server.

## Operation Lifecycle Rules

- Every device operation runs under the process-wide `OperationRegistry` (`api/src/operations.ts`). It owns the single `ResourceArbiter` and `OperationCoordinator` of `nebulosa`, plus a `DeviceLifecycle` that follows every managed device type.
- An operation holds its devices by physical identity (`resourceKey`, the hardware id, so the interfaces of one device, such as a camera and its integrated filter wheel, are one resource). A second operation on a held device is refused as busy, whichever service or tab starts it; nothing waits or queues. Disconnecting or removing a device cancels the operations holding it. An immediate setting (see Equipment Control Contract) holds its device through the coordinator for the command only and publishes no record.
- Each start, progress, and end is published as `operation:updated` (`OperationRecord`: id, `OperationKind`, exact devices, state, start instant, latest progress, failure reason and error); an operation is published as running before its work starts, so a progress reported from its first synchronous step is published too. An operation started for a command token is remembered by it (the newest 256), so a repeated token answers the same operation. `GET /api/operations` lists the running ones. The web mirrors them in `web/src/operations.ts`.
- `OperationRecorder` (`api/src/operationhistory.ts`) keeps the history of the operations in `operation_runs`: `publish` of `api/src/index.ts` hands it every event through the `ServerEventDispatcher`, and it writes the start of an operation and its end (state and reason), with the names its devices had, never a progress nor the text of an error, which can hold server paths. An operation still running when the server stopped is recorded as `interrupted` when it starts again, and nothing resumes it. Rows older than 90 days or beyond the newest 10 000 are deleted as operations end. `GET /api/operations/history?before=&limit=` pages it newest first (at most 200 per page, `next` the start instant to ask before); no screen shows it yet.
- Operations are owned by the server. Switching workspace, profile, camera tab, or mode, collapsing a panel, or unmounting a component never ends one.
- A server stopped by a signal (`app/src/main.ts`) first cancels every running operation and waits, at most 3 s (`stopOperations` of `api/src/index.ts`, `OperationRegistry.stop`), for their cleanups to stop what they commanded, then closes the database: a driver keeps a motion once its client is gone, so exiting at once would leave a mount moving under a held nudge. A second signal ends the process at once. A hot reload of `bun dev` evaluates the server code again in the same process, which kept the previous run alive beside the new one (its device connections, the operations commanding the devices, its listeners, and its database); the new run first stops the previous one in place (`stopServer`: the operations as above, then every service holding devices, sockets, listeners, timers, or workers, the connections, and the database) and opens again the connections it had open (`reopenConnections`). Operations and in-memory sessions do not survive a reload; the connections are closed and opened again.
- Interruptions are distinct commands, offered only when supported: cancel a pending request, abort an exposure, stop guiding, stop mount motion, stop a persistent service. There is no generic "Cancel": the Abort of the status bar sends the command of each operation, the same as the page that started it (see Status bar).
- Long-lived services (guiding sessions, Live Stacking sessions, Mount Remote Control listeners, Meridian Flip Monitors) are server objects with explicit start and stop and their own state events. They are not operations of a React screen.
- A service composing tools inside the process runs them under a `ToolInvocation` (`api/src/operations.ts`): a `ResourceReservation` of the registry's arbiter holding every device the tool commands, kept in process memory only (never in a request, an event, a store, or the database). `admits(device, invocation)` grants a tool a device, without an invocation, only when it is available and, with one, only when that reservation holds it and no operation of it holds it now (`reserves` tells whether the reservation holds it at all), so a tool never takes a device outside the reservation it was given and a manual command stays refused as busy on a reserved device; `scope(invocation)` is the coordinator or the reservation's scope (`reservedScope`) every operation of the tool starts with. `admit` starts the operation of a token as `run` does and returns an `OperationAdmission`: the running record, `finished` (the final record, never rejecting), and whether the token had already started it, so a repeated token answers the same end. `admitCommand` of `api/src/commands.ts` is the in-process form of `startCommand`, answering the admission or a `CommandRefusal` (status, code, message) a route answers as `{ error, code }`. Stopping a device (`stopResponse`, the mount stop) still cancels whatever operation holds it, reserved or not: a stop is a safety command. Only the services the Capture–Sequencer Compatibility Report lists as ready accept an invocation; every other operation is manual and refused on a reserved device. Camera Acquisition reserves the mount and the guide output of its Mount dithering for the whole capture (`api/src/dithering.ts`); no other caller reserves devices yet besides `api/src/composition.test.ts`.
- Toolkit runners (the `GuiderClient` of a guiding session, `CameraCapturer` reports) keep their own state machines. The registry tracks what they hold; it does not replace their states.
- The shared `CameraCapturer` of `api/src/captures.ts` (Camera Capture, Auto Focus, TPPA, DARV, Mount Pointing, Target Centering, Flat Wizard, Dark Library Wizard) allows `FRAME_TRANSFER_TIMEOUT` (10 min) for a frame's payload to arrive after its exposure, and for a stopped exposure to stop being Busy. INDI delivers in order, so a stop is answered only after the BLOB in transit, and an unbinned frame of a large sensor over a slow link takes minutes (an ASI294MM Pro frame of 124.8 MB in base64 took about 130 s at 1.2 MB/s). The camera stays held until then, and a frame that is really lost fails only after that time.

## Device Resource Matrix

Requirements are declared by each tool's registry; the web evaluates availability with `roleAvailability` and `deviceAvailability` (`web/src/equipment/availability.ts`): ready, busy (a running operation holds that exact connection and device id), unsupported, or the role or device state that prevents use. Unsupported is a connected device the operation can never run on, judged by the operation from the device state the server reports (mechanical design, capabilities) and passed as its reason; it comes before busy, and Equipment Setup is its fix. A device reserved by a caller composing tools (see Operation Lifecycle Rules) shows ready while no operation runs on it, and the server still refuses it to every manual operation.

Every `OperationKind` and the devices it holds:

| Feature                         | Operation kinds                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Requires (besides the view's camera)                 | Holds                                                                                                                             |
| ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Camera Acquisition              | `camera.capture`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | —                                                    | camera, until its last frame arrived; with Mount dithering, a reservation of the mount and the guide output for the whole capture |
| Live Stacking                   | none                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | —                                                    | nothing: it integrates the frames of Camera Acquisition                                                                           |
| Mount Nudge                     | `mount.nudge`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Mount                                                | mount, only while a direction is held                                                                                             |
| Guiding                         | `camera.guiding`, `guideOutput.guiding`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Guide output                                         | camera for the whole session; the guide output's physical device (the mount, or the camera of its ST4 port) only while guiding    |
| Auto Focus                      | `focuser.autofocus`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Focuser                                              | camera and focuser for the whole session                                                                                          |
| DARV                            | `mount.darv`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Mount                                                | camera and mount during each measurement, free between them                                                                       |
| TPPA                            | `mount.tppa`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Mount; a plate solver                                | camera and mount for the whole session                                                                                            |
| Mount pointing session          | `mount.pointingModel`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Mount; a plate solver; the camera is the Main Camera | camera and mount for each stretch of work, free while paused                                                                      |
| Mount pointing verification     | `mount.pointingVerification`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Mount; a plate solver                                | camera and mount for the whole test                                                                                               |
| Target Centering                | `mount.targetCentering`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Mount; a plate solver                                | camera for the whole session; mount from when the guiding it stopped released it until the end                                    |
| Flat Wizard                     | `camera.flatWizard`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | — (filter wheel, flat panel, and cover optional)     | camera; the wheel, panel, and cover it uses, for the whole session, also while it waits for the operator                          |
| Dark Library Wizard             | `camera.darkLibraryWizard`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | — (filter wheel, flat panel, and cover optional)     | camera; the wheel, panel, and cover its protection selected, for the whole session, also while paused                             |
| Sky slew                        | `mount.slew`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Mount                                                | mount until the slew ends                                                                                                         |
| Mount Remote Control            | `mount.goto`, `mount.remoteMove`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Mount (no camera)                                    | mount during a client's goto or manual motion; for the command only during a sync or site change, which publish no record         |
| Meridian Flip Monitor           | none                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Mount (no camera)                                    | nothing: it reads the mount and starts no operation                                                                               |
| Equipment Control motion        | `mount.goto`, `mount.flip`, `mount.park`, `mount.unpark`, `mount.home`, `mount.findHome`, `focuser.move`, `wheel.move`, `rotator.move`, `rotator.home`, `cover.open`, `cover.close`, `dome.slew`, `dome.home`, `dome.park`, `dome.unpark`, `dome.shutter`, `guideOutput.pulse` | the device shown                                     | the physical device until the motion ends                                                                                         |
| Equipment Control dome rotation | `dome.move`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    | the device shown                                     | the dome, only while a direction is held                                                                                          |
| Equipment Control setting       | none                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | the device shown                                     | the physical device, for the command only                                                                                         |
| INDI property write             | `indi.propertyWrite`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | the device shown                                     | the physical device until the driver ends the change, only while connected                                                        |

Each feature conflicts with every operation holding one of its devices, immediate settings included (cooling during a capture, a panel's light during a Flat Wizard), and with nothing else but one case: Target Centering stops the guiding that may pulse its mount and restores it afterwards, refuses to start beside guiding it would not stop, and keeps guiding from starting until it ends, since a guide output on a camera's ST4 port shares no device with the mount. Camera Acquisition with Mount dithering likewise refuses to start beside guiding on the connections of its mount and guide output and keeps guiding from starting there until it ends, and refuses a guide output that is its own camera. Conflicts are resolved by the arbiter on the server, never only in the browser, because several tabs (and profiles) may address the same device.

## Equipment Control Contract

- The page lists the devices of the active profile's connection only, grouped by hardware id, so the interfaces of one physical device (a flip-flat's cover and flat panel, a mount and its guide output) appear once. The INDI directory adds every device the connection reports that no interface manages, named in the URL by its hardware id (`web/src/equipment-control/groups.ts`). Each device also has read-only Diagnostics (`DeviceDiagnostics.tsx`, collapsed): the identity and driver of each interface, the capabilities it declares, its last errors, the operations holding it, and its raw state. Opening it, switching devices, or reloading never connects, disconnects, or commands anything.
- Every command goes through `api` under the shared contract of `api/src/commands.ts`: settings answer 204 once the driver reports the commanded state (504 `timeout` when it never does), motions answer 202 with an operation id and report their outcome only through `operation:updated`. Durable requests carry a client token, so a repeated request never moves a device twice; the web generates tokens and ids with `nanoid`, never `crypto.randomUUID`, which browsers only provide in a secure context (HTTPS or localhost) and the app is usually opened over plain HTTP on the local network.
- Stops never wait for a lease: `POST …/stop` cancels the operation of the physical device and sends the commander's `stopMotion` (`stopResponse` of `api/src/commands.ts`). A filter wheel has no abort. Guide pulse stops never cancel another operation of the mount.
- Manual motion held by a lease (mount nudge, dome rotation) shares `api/src/leases.ts` and `web/src/press.ts`.
- The state of a camera also says whether it is exposing (`exposing`), for whichever tool asked for the frame; the progress of an exposure stays with the capture that runs it, so this field changes only when an exposure starts or ends.
- The sensor temperature of a camera (`CCD_TEMPERATURE`) or a focuser (`FOCUS_TEMPERATURE`) is read by the toolkit's `ThermometerManager`, registered in `DeviceRegistry` after the managed types, and published in the parent's state (`temperature` of `camera:capabilities` and `focuser:updated`); its thermometer proxies are not a managed type. A camera reports a `cooler` only when it has a cooler switch or a settable target temperature, so a camera with a bare thermometer shows only its temperature.
- The mechanical type of a mount (`mountType`) comes from `TELESCOPE_MOUNT_TYPE` or the Alpaca `alignmentmode`, and is `UNKNOWN` until reported; only `ALTAZ` is evidence against a polar alignment, and the INDI base class reports `EQ_GEM` unless its driver says otherwise.
- Known driver differences: Alpaca mounts expose only Go Home (no find or set home, no set park, no flip); Alpaca domes cannot unpark or rotate manually; Alpaca focusers have no sync or reverse; Alpaca rotators have no home. Pier side and the INDI Alignment Subsystem are reported but have no commander, so the controls show them read-only; power, weather, safety monitor, and GPS devices are not managed. The vectors of all of them can still be seen and written in INDI Properties (see INDI Properties Contract). The simulator has no composite cover with a heater; that path is tested with a fake client.

## INDI Properties Contract

- The raw property vectors of every device of every connection, managed or not, are kept once in `DeviceRegistry.properties`, the toolkit's `IndiPropertyRegistry`: first in the handler of every client, shared by every device manager, which reads it instead of keeping its own copy. It holds copies of the vectors with their definition metadata, latest state, values, ranges, message, and timestamp, and a revision per vector; never BLOB bytes, only the format and size of the latest delivery.
- `IndiPropertyService` (`api/src/indi.ts`) publishes it for every connection: an `indiserver` speaks INDI natively, and the simulator and the Alpaca client define and update the vectors of their devices and take new values through the same `Client` commands (the Alpaca client translates the vectors it maps into Alpaca calls and ignores writes to the others, which then time out). The directory (`GET /api/connections/:id/indi/devices`, `IndiDeviceSummary`) lists each physical device once by hardware id, including drivers no manager supports, with its driver identification and the connection flag its `CONNECTION` vector reports, never a value; its changes are broadcast as `indi:deviceAdded`, `indi:deviceUpdated`, and `indi:deviceRemoved`.
- The property stream of one physical device (`connectionId`, `hardwareId`) reaches only the sockets that sent `indi:subscribe` for it: an `indi:snapshot` (every vector in driver order and the latest 20 driver messages), then `indi:propertyDefined`, `indi:propertyUpdated` (the changed elements with the vector's current state, message, timestamp, and timeout), `indi:propertyRemoved`, and `indi:message`. A refused subscription gets `indi:subscriptionError` (`notFound`). Subscribing again resends the snapshot; `indi:unsubscribe`, the close of the socket, or the removal of the device end it at once, with no grace period.
- While a device has no subscriber its changes build no payload, serialize nothing, and start no timer. Value-only changes of a vector are coalesced and sent every `VALUE_INTERVAL` (250 ms) with the latest values; definitions, removals, and changes of state, message, range, or timeout are sent at once, carrying the values still pending for that vector. Each stream event carries the `epoch` of its connection's client (new on every reconnection) and a `revision` increasing across the service; each vector carries its own revision. A socket with more than `MAX_BUFFERED_AMOUNT` (1 MiB) queued, or that queued or dropped a send, skips its streams until it drains, then receives a fresh snapshot of each.
- A write (`POST /api/connections/:id/indi/devices/:hardwareId/properties/:vector`, `IndiWriteRequest`) names the revision of the vector the tab shows and is refused as `conflict` (409, with the current `vector`) when the vector changed since; it writes only text, number, and switch vectors the driver does not mark `ro` (`unsupported` otherwise), names only elements the vector has, and sends the whole vector: text and number elements left out keep their current values in a `rw` vector and must be given in a `wo` one, switches are sent as the complete selection, left-out ones off except in a readable `AnyOfMany` vector, with exactly one on for `OneOfMany` and at most one for `AtMostOne` (`invalidParameter` otherwise). Values are not checked against the declared ranges, which the driver applies. A write of `ON_COORD_SET`, `EQUATORIAL_EOD_COORD`, `GEOGRAPHIC_COORD`, or `TIME_UTC` (`MODEL_SENSITIVE_VECTORS`) suspends the pointing model in force on that mount once the write is admitted; a write refused, as busy or otherwise, leaves it in force (see Mount Pointing Model Contract). It runs as an `indi.propertyWrite` operation under the command contract (202, token, outcome through `operation:updated`). While the device reports being connected it holds the physical device, so it is refused as `busy` while another operation holds it and is cancelled when the device disconnects; a device that is not connected is written without a hold, so its port or `CONNECTION` can be set.
- A write ends with the first update of its vector after it was sent that is not `Busy`: `Ok` or `Idle` succeed, `Alert` fails as `alert` with the driver's message. It times out after the vector's `timeout` plus `WRITE_GRACE` (5 s), 60 s when none is declared and at most 600 s, saying the device may still be working; the deletion or redefinition of the vector meanwhile ends it as `commandFailed` (unknown result), and the removal of the device as `removed`.
- The web view (`web/src/equipment-control/indi/`) is the INDI Properties view of a device in Equipment Control, `/equipment/<device id>/indi`: a device both managed and INDI chooses between Controls and INDI Properties with a selector, a device no interface manages only has INDI Properties. `indiDirectory` mirrors the directory through `SERVER_STORES`; `indiView` is not a server store but the stream of the one device shown: the view subscribes while mounted, its device is in the directory, and the page is visible (a hidden page pauses the subscription and keeps what is shown and the drafts), subscribes again on every socket open, drops events of another epoch or older than what it applied, and unsubscribes when it unmounts or changes device. Vectors are grouped by the driver's groups in driver order (unnamed ones under Other), searchable by group, vector, and element names and labels, in collapsible groups, the first open at first. Text and number vectors are edited in fields (numbers in decimal or sexagesimal, in the driver's units and format, never rounded while typing; write-only fields start empty) and applied with Apply, which sends the edited elements only with the revision shown; `OneOfMany` switches use a select and Apply, `AnyOfMany` checkboxes and Apply, `AtMostOne` action buttons that send at once with an explicit Clear; lights and BLOB metadata are read-only. A draft is never replaced by what the device reports, which is noted as a change on the device, is kept until its write succeeds, and stays after a refusal (a conflict shows the current vector) or a failure; a redefinition of the vector or a new epoch discards it. Each vector shows the outcome of its latest write from `operation:updated`, waiting while the driver works.
- Limitations: success only means the driver ended the change; an update already on its way when the change was sent ends the write too, and a driver may report `Ok` while its hardware still moves, which then no longer holds the device. A driver message without a device, or for a device not seen yet, is not kept.

## Sky Map Contract

- One tick drives the star map and the selection (`#followClock` of `web/src/sky/planetarium.ts`): every `SKY_REALTIME_TICK_MS` (15 s) while the clock follows the real time, every 250 ms while playing, and once for a fixed instant. Each tick reads the instant once, shows it on the map, and refreshes the selection's position and events for that same instant (`refreshSkySelection` with the tick's time): accurately on every real-time tick, from the local models every 2 s while playing, and accurately 400 ms after a fixed instant settles. The time bar's own redraws are display only. Once the clock is not playing, an instant more than `MAP_DATA_EPOCH_MS` (a Julian year) from the map data's reloads the catalogs (`mapDataCovers` of `web/src/sky/sky.ts`).
- Picking tolerates fingers: a touch or pen tap picks within `touchPickRadius` (22 px) and stays a tap within `touchTapSlop` (10 px of travel), a mouse click within `pickRadius` (7 px) and `tapSlop` (3 px) (`InteractionOptions` of `web/src/sky/celestial/`).
- On the local sky zoomed in to `SKY_ANCHOR_ZOOM` or more, a tick that changes the instant keeps the view on the same point of the sky (recentered after the update) instead of the same point of the horizon, unless the mount is followed; at lower zoom the horizon stays still.
- Selected objects the map data lacks are remembered for the tab (`RememberedObjects` of `web/src/sky/remembered.ts`, at most `REMEMBERED_LIMIT` = 32, the least recently selected forgotten first, in memory only, kept when the map is replaced by the other kind of chart): minor bodies from SBDB as moving bodies drawn at every zoom whatever their magnitude (`alwaysVisible`), and catalog stars and deep-sky objects fainter than the map data as selectable shapes with their name. They stay drawn and can be selected again on the map. The selected one follows the Inspector's position of each tick; the other minor bodies are moved with local (`fast`) positions at every map update. Satellites are never remembered: the satellite layer draws them (see Artificial Satellites Contract).
- The projection of the map is fixed: the local sky is centred on the observer's zenith and the equatorial chart on its own hemisphere, and no feature ever moves the projection centre or reprojects the map around a coordinate. "Centre on" a coordinate (the mount, a coordinate or footprint sent from an image) only pans the screen view with `Planetarium.centerOn`/`Celestial.centerOnCoordinate`, keeping the zoom and orientation; a point the map cannot project (below the local horizon, or outside the hemisphere the equatorial chart projects) leaves the view unchanged and is said to be so.
- Handoffs from a solved image (`web/src/sky/footprint.ts`, `skyImageState`, tab memory): Open Coordinates in Sky (the image context menu, and the details of a marker) marks a position and pans to it; Show Footprint in Sky (Images → Plate Solver) draws the outline the selected solution gives to the image borders (24 points per side, broken where the WCS cannot unproject) as a dashed shape of its own beside the framing, with a cross at its centre, and pans to that centre. Both keep their positions in the J2000 (ICRS or FK5 J2000) frame of the solution and are converted for the map with the toolkit's IAU 2006/2000A precession-nutation matrix at the instant the catalogs were computed for (`ofDateMatrix`, the conversion of `api/src/sky/catalog.ts`), so they line up with the stars drawn; the matrix is computed once per map data load, never per tick, and nothing is requested. A footprint is drawn only while its document still shows that image and is dropped otherwise (the document closed, or its camera tab shows another frame); the mark stays until replaced or cleared from the Sky toolbar, where a toggle also hides the footprint and shows it again in place, without moving the view (sending a footprint shows it). Neither changes the Sky selection, the clock, or the mount.
- The search reads Bayer and Flamsteed designations by the IAU abbreviation or the genitive of the constellation and the Greek letter by name, symbol, or abbreviation, with SIMBAD's own (`alf`, `tet`, `ksi`) too, so "Alpha Centauri", "alf Ori", and "58 Orionis" all resolve. Catalog stars stop at magnitude 8 (`bin/src/sky.catalog.ts`), which the empty result says.
- `POST /api/sky/position` returns the rise, transit, and set for an observer in both modes (they are computed locally and cached per hour), so the Visibility of the Object Inspector always shows the instant of its Position. Rise and set are the instants the airless altitude crosses the standard horizon: −34′ for a point, −50′ for the Sun, and −49.5′ for the Moon, its mean semidiameter below the horizon of a point, on its topocentric place. Transit is the crossing of the meridian, the hour angle going from negative to positive, not the highest point, with its altitude read from the same model. Horizontal altitudes hold the refraction of a standard atmosphere (ERFA from 5°, Sæmundsson below, fading out under the horizon).
- Every request of the process to JPL (Horizons, SBDB, CAD) goes through one `SsdRequestQueue` (`api/src/ssd.ts`), since its fair-use policy allows one request at a time: tasks run in order, the budget of each starts with it, the wait for a turn is limited to `SSD_QUEUE_WAIT_MS` (15 s), identical requests are shared, and the last caller to leave stops one. Requests name the client in a `User-Agent` with the version `app/src/main.ts` gives to `identifyJplClient` and the repository as contact. A 429 or 503 is never retried: it pauses the queue for its `Retry-After`, 30 s by default and 5 minutes at most, and the queue refuses tasks meanwhile; only SBDB and CAD answers can do so, since the Horizons adapter does not expose the status. An accurate position may therefore wait behind another JPL request, and a refusal of the queue falls back to the local model with `fallbackReason: 'timeout'` without counting against the Horizons circuit breaker.

## Sun Inspector Contract

- Solar almanac (`api/src/sky/sun.ts`, `POST /api/sky/sun/almanac`): a stateless, read-only computation from the toolkit, never the network. For an instant (Unix milliseconds), an observer, and an IANA time zone it answers the Sun's day on the observer's civil date of that instant (`localDay`, absent without an observer), the next four equinoxes and solstices strictly after the instant (named `marchEquinox`, `juneSolstice`, `septemberEquinox`, `decemberSolstice`, so the names hold in both hemispheres), and the equation of time and the Carrington rotation in progress at the instant.
- The civil date is the time zone's, with its real bounds (`zonedDay`): a day the clocks change is 23 or 25 hours long, and a skipped midnight starts the day when the clocks jump. Sunrise, transit, sunset, the daylight (the time above the sunrise threshold within the day), the civil, nautical, and astronomical twilight, and the astronomical night (from the day's astronomical dusk to the next astronomical dawn within 36 hours) use the toolkit's almanac convention: the geometric, geocentric altitude of the Sun's center against `SUN_HORIZON` (−50′) and −6°, −12°, and −18°. This differs by seconds from the rise and set of `POST /api/sky/position` (the upper limb against the refracted topocentric altitude, nearest events within ±12 h), which other objects keep.
- A threshold the Sun crosses both ways is `normal`, one way `partial`, and not at all `continuousAbove` or `continuousBelow`; the first upward and the last downward crossings are kept. Absent crossings, daylight, and durations are absent, never 0. The equation of time uses the Sun's apparent geocentric right ascension of date (nutation and aberration), never the observer's topocentric one. Local days are cached (the latest 64 by date, zone, and observer).
- Observation sources: the latest SDO image (`api/src/sky/sdo.ts`, `GET /api/sky/sun/image?channel&size`) and the latest NOAA SWPC active region report (`api/src/sky/activeregions.ts`, `GET /api/sky/sun/active-regions`) are current real-world observations, never tied to the Sky clock. The image is one of the fixed public JPEGs of the SDO `latest` directory, chosen from an allowlist of channels (`aia193`, `aia171`, `aia304`, `hmiIntensitygram` = HMI colored intensitygram, `hmiMagnetogram`) and sizes (512, 1024), so no URL is built from a request. SDO tells only when a file was modified (`Last-Modified`), never when it was observed, so the route answers that time and the download time (`X-Sun-Fetched-At`) as headers, and the client calls it the update time. The NOAA feed keeps a month of daily records; the report is the records of its latest observed date, one per region, with null fields absent; its spot counts are per group, never the international sunspot number.
- Both sources go through `RemoteCache` (`api/src/sky/remote.ts`): fresh for 15 minutes (images) or 30 minutes (report), one download shared by concurrent requests, a 15 s timeout, a 4 MB limit, and a content type check. When a download fails the last good value is answered marked stale (`X-Sun-Stale: 1`, `stale: true`), without downloading again for a minute; with none, `502 upstreamUnavailable`. A failure of one source never affects the other nor the almanac.
- Sun Inspector (`web/src/sky/sun/`): the Object Inspector of the body `10` (`SunInspector`, dispatched by `ObjectInspector`); no Sky panel view or navigation entry of its own, and no lunar data. Under the name its magnitude and the latest SDO image (`SolarImage`: a channel selector, the update and download times in UTC, a stale image said to be one, a larger 1024 px image in a `Modal` on a click), then the actions of every object (`ObjectActions`), then Active Regions (one row per region, its details on a click), Position with the apparent angular diameter (`2·asin(SUN_RADIUS_AU / distance)`, the distance of the position shown), Visibility (refracted altitude and azimuth of the position, then the sunrise, transit, sunset, and daylight of the almanac's local day), Twilight, Next Solar Eclipse, Seasons (the hemisphere's season named only with an observer), and Solar Physical Data.
- Three times are kept apart: the position and the almanac follow the instant the Sky shows (`selection.position.time`), the day is the site's civil date of that instant in the site's own zone (no day without a site), whatever zone the times are shown in (the profile's display zone), and the image and the report are the latest real observations, dated by their sources, whatever the clock shows. The store (`sun.ts`, `sunState`, tab memory) asks for the almanac only when the observer, the zone, the civil date, or the 10-minute bucket of the instant changes, or the instant passes the next season; for the next eclipse (`/api/eclipses/solar?direction=next`, and `/:id/local` with an observer) only when the instant leaves the interval from the search instant to the eclipse's maximum or the observer changes; and for the image and the report on mount and every 15 minutes while shown. Each resource has its own request guard, loading, and error. A value of another day, zone, or observer is cleared at once. View Eclipse Map opens `/tools/solar-eclipse-map/<eclipse id>` of the eclipse shown. Slew to Object is the generic action: nothing refuses pointing at the Sun, in the inspector or in `api`.

## Moon Inspector Contract

- Lunar almanac (`api/src/sky/moon.ts`, `POST /api/sky/moon/almanac`): a stateless, read-only computation from the toolkit, never the network. For an instant (Unix milliseconds) and an optional observer it answers the Moon's appearance (`MoonAppearance`), the next four principal phases strictly after the instant in time order, and the next perigee and apogee strictly after it with their geocentric distance (km) and apparent diameter. The appearance is topocentric with an observer and geocentric without one: the illuminated fraction and phase angle, the phase name (a principal phase within ±6.1°, half a day of mean synodic motion, of its geocentric Moon−Sun ecliptic longitude difference, otherwise crescent or gibbous, waxing below 180°), the age since the previous geocentric new Moon, the Brown lunation number, distance, apparent diameter (IAU mean radius 1737.4 km), horizontal parallax, the sub-observer point (optical, physical, and with an observer diurnal libration) and sub-solar point (east longitude in (−π, π]), the Sun's selenographic colongitude, the position angles of the pole and of the bright limb, and with an observer the parallactic angle. `bodyToCamera` is the one rotation a renderer needs: from the Moon's IAU body-fixed frame at the light-time retarded instant to a north-up view frame (+X celestial west, +Y celestial north of date, +Z toward the observer), composed from the light-time corrected observer→Moon direction and `bodyFixedMatrix(MOON_ROTATION)`; `sunDirection` is the Moon→Sun unit vector in the body-fixed frame. A zenith-up view is the north-up one rotated by −parallacticAngle. The Moon is ELPMPP02 with VSOP87E for the Earth and the Sun and the IAU rotation model, and the phases and apsides are Meeus' series, so the values may differ slightly from the Horizons-sourced `SkyPosition` of the selection, whose illuminated fraction is the Position section's. The calendar is cached by the interval in which it holds (from the previous new Moon until the first event), so it is computed again after an event or when the instant moves back; the appearance is computed for the exact instant every time.
- Moon Inspector (`web/src/sky/moon/`): the Object Inspector of the body `301` (`MoonInspector`, dispatched by `ObjectInspector`); no Sky panel view or navigation entry of its own. Under the name its magnitude when known and a drawing of the Moon (`MoonImage`), its phase name and illuminated fraction, and how it is oriented ("From the observer · Zenith up", or "From the Earth's center · North up" without an observer), the NASA credit, and a larger view in a `Modal` on a click (the Lunar Surface Explorer below); then Show Framing and Slew to Object. Then Position (with the apparent angular diameter from the position's distance), Visibility (the generic `ObserverSection`: altitude, azimuth, rise, transit, set), Lunar Phases (`lunarPhases`), Perigee & Apogee (`lunarApsides`), Next Lunar Eclipse (`eclipse`: type, greatest eclipse, magnitude, the local visibility of `/api/eclipses/lunar/:id/local` with an observer, and View Lunar Eclipse Map, which opens `/tools/lunar-eclipse-map/<eclipse id>`), and Lunar Physical Data (`physical`: age, lunation, distance, apparent diameter, libration, colongitude, sub-solar latitude, pole and bright-limb position angles, parallax, parallactic angle).
- The drawing (`MoonCanvas`, `moon.renderer.ts`) is the texture on an orthographic sphere through `moon.geometry.ts`: the API's `bodyToCamera`, turned by −parallacticAngle for zenith up, a viewport in CSS pixels, and a soft terminator from `sunDirection` with a faint earthshine; the shading is illustrative, not photometric. WebGL2 draws it with one fragment shader that inverts the projection per pixel, only when the appearance, orientation, size, or view changes; without WebGL2 the same functions run on the CPU at up to 768 pixels on a side, so the drawing and any marker drawn through `moon.geometry.ts` stay aligned. A lost WebGL context is restored and drawn again; the renderer is disposed with the canvas. The store (`moon.ts`, `moonState`, tab memory) asks for the almanac at every new instant of the selection (the Sky's own refresh cadence) and keeps the last drawing of the same observer faded while the next loads, clears it at once for another observer, discards late answers through request guards, and asks for the eclipse only when the instant leaves the interval its search covers; an almanac error never hides the eclipse, and the reverse.
- Lunar Surface Explorer (`MoonImageModal`, the larger view of the Moon drawing): the 4K map zoomed with the wheel or a pinch (1× to 32×) and panned by dragging (`celestial/gesture.ts`), Zenith up / North up, a category filter (all, craters, maria and plains, mountains, rilles and valleys, other), names on or off, and Whole Moon. The catalog (`moon.features.json.gz`) is fetched and unzipped on the first opening, kept out of the proxy (`moonState.features`), and its absence or failure only disables the names. Markers (`MoonFeaturesOverlay`, `featureMarkers` in `moon.features.ts`) are SVG dots at the official center points drawn through the same view matrix and viewport as the texture, so they stay aligned under zoom, pan, orientation, libration, and time; points on the far hemisphere are never drawn, and those on the night side are dimmed. A feature is drawn when its catalog dimension spans at least 16 CSS pixels (20 for satellite craters), by priority (apparent size times a category weight favoring maria and mountains), at most 300 at once, and labels go to markers whose label box overlaps no label or marker of a higher priority, at most 80, on the right of the marker or on its left near the edge; the selected feature is always drawn and labeled. The search ignores case and diacritics, lists names starting with the text first and visible features before those turned away (marked "Not visible now"), and centers and zooms on a visible result. The details (`MoonFeatureDetails`) give the official name, descriptor, center, the approximate diameter or, for elongated features, length, the approval year, the origin of the name, whether the center is on the visible hemisphere and in daylight now (a feature that turns away keeps its details), a link to its Gazetteer page, and the NASA and USGS/IAU credits. Zoom, pan, filter, names, search, and selection are presentation state of the tab (`moonState.explorer`, the view's own transform); none changes the observer or the instant. Center points are markers, never outlines or measured extents; no elevation is shown.
- Moon assets (`web/assets/moon/`, generated by `bun make:moon` from `bin/src/moon.assets.ts`, not committed): `moon.2k.jpg` is the NASA SVS CGI Moon Kit LROC color map (https://svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/4720/, 2025 version) copied unchanged at 2048×1024; `moon.4k.jpg` is its 16-bit sRGB 4096×2048 TIFF reduced to 8 bits and encoded with the toolkit's TurboJPEG (about 1.9 MB). Both are equirectangular, centered on 0° longitude, east to the right, north up, and illustrative: NASA optimizes them for appearance, not science, and fills latitudes beyond ±70° with LOLA albedo. `moon.features.json.gz` (the gzipped JSON `MoonFeatureCatalog` of `api/src/sky/moon.features.ts`, about 260 KB, 1.2 MB unzipped) holds every IAU-adopted lunar surface feature of the USGS Gazetteer of Planetary Nomenclature center-point archive (`MOON_nomenclature_center_pts.zip`, about 9 100 names) as positional rows: Gazetteer id, official name with its diacritics, official descriptor, explorer category, satellite and elongated flags, planetocentric latitude and east longitude in degrees in [-180, 180) (converted once from the archive's [0, 360)), the approximate diameter or longest extent in km, the approval year, and the origin of the name. Center points are markers, never outlines. `moon.provenance.json` records each source's URL, SHA-256, size, and download time, each output, and the credits. The generator reads the archive's ZIP, DBF, and the LZW TIFF itself (`bin/src/moon/`), keeps only the status `Adopted by IAU`, fails on an approval status or descriptor it does not know, and writes the records it leaves out and why to `bin/data/moon/moon.features.audit.json`. Sources are downloaded once into `bin/data/moon/` (not committed); updating the assets is deleting them there and running `bun make:moon --force`, never a refresh at run time; outputs whose bytes are unchanged are left alone, and a changed one is replaced only with the development server stopped, since Windows refuses to replace a file it serves. `bun make:moon --empty` writes a gray 2×1 map and an empty catalog without downloading anything.

## Sky Framing Integration Contract

- Delivered: Framing is a panel view of Sky (`web/src/sky/FramingPanel.tsx`) that draws the field of view around the selection (`syncFraming` of `planetarium.ts`, the gnomonic outline of `framePolygon`) from an `OpticalPreset` or the Equipment Setup, resolved by `resolveFraming` of `web/src/sky/framing.ts`: angular size, image scale, and position angle measured from north through east. Its Equipment Setup source combines the sensor of a camera role or a manual one with the optics that camera looks through (`usesGuideTelescope`): the profile's guide telescope (`guideTelescope`) for the Guide Camera, its telescope otherwise; the panel edits those optics under their name.
- Planned, nothing built yet: survey images from `hips2fits` (`core/src/adapters/sky/hips2fits.ts`) through an `api` proxy route that caches cutouts and survey lists and returns the survey's attribution. Network failure leaves the planetarium framing usable.
- The cutout is to be requested in TAN projection centered on the framing center, with the field and rotation of the framing, so its pixels match the outline of `framePolygon`. The first task is a test that pins the sign convention of the position angle against a cutout's WCS.
- Pixels are not assumed to be linear in RA/Dec. Any overlay on the survey uses the cutout's WCS.

## Tools Integration Contract

- Navigation: Tools → catalog card → `/tools/<id>` (`TOOL_DEFINITIONS` of `web/src/tools/tools.ts`), rendered by the tool's `view` inside its own `WorkspaceLayout`. A tool without a view (the Alpaca Server) is planned and its URL is replaced by the catalog's. The Astro Weather, Calibration Library, Image Stacking, and Mount Pointing Model tools follow the contracts of their own sections.
- Calculator (`web/src/tools/calculator/`): task-oriented calculators chosen in one menu grouped by category (`catalog.ts`), each composing pure formulas of `nebulosa/src/astronomy/formulas` behind one form, never one entry per formula and no expression engine. The five categories are Optics & Field of View (Imaging Setup: field, diagonal, image scale, effective focal length and ratio through `resolveFraming`, so it matches Sky Framing's 2·atan convention, with no image scale for a sensor given by its physical size; Mosaic Estimator: rectangular panel count per axis with overlap, not an optimized layout), Sampling & Focus (Sampling: FWHM in pixels from the seeing or the seeing from a measured FWHM, the 2–3 px rule-of-thumb class, and the focal length for a target FWHM; Diffraction & Focus: Airy disk and critical focus zone, wavelength in nanometers), Exposure & Integration (Exposure Planner: total integration from a frame count or whole frames for a target time, with the idealized √N and 1.25·log₁₀N stacking gains; Star Trailing: sidereal drift of an untracked star and the longest exposure within a trail budget, none at a celestial pole), Visual Observation (Eyepiece & Telescope: magnification, exit pupil, true field from the apparent field or the field stop, Dawes and Rayleigh limits, limiting magnitude; Aperture Comparison: clear light grasp and the collecting area left by central obstructions, never presented as resolution), and Atmosphere & Conditions (Airmass & Visibility Effects: Kasten–Young airmass, extinction for a coefficient, Sæmundsson refraction; Dew Conditions: Magnus dew point with the heuristic ambient-margin risk, humidity from a dew point, or frost point from a humidity relative to ice). Each calculator shows its formula, units, domain, and approximations; mode switches fix the direction of a calculation so fields never depend on each other circularly. Camera & Signal and Guiding & Tracking calculators are not provided yet. Everything runs in the browser: no route, event, operation, or device is involved. Fields keep their text as typed and are parsed only at the calculation boundary (`values.ts`), so an empty or out-of-domain field shows what to enter or why it cannot be used instead of a previous result, and no result shows NaN or Infinity. Each calculator keeps a draft in React state while the tool is open; drafts start from labeled examples and are never persisted, and a change of active profile starts over. An optical configuration can be imported by value (`sources.ts`) from the Main or Guide Camera (the telescope or guide telescope with the sensor that camera reports, never another camera's), from the Sky Framing manual sensor (refused when none is entered), or from an optical preset; the draft is then edited on its own, live changes never rewrite it, its origin is shown only while the same camera is followed, and the profile, presets, and devices are never written.
