- Astronomical Time Converter (`api/src/astronomicaltime.ts`, `astronomicaltime.eop.ts`, `web/src/tools/astronomicaltime/`): stateless, read-only conversions of one instant, never operations or events, with the toolkit as the only source of astronomy: no value crosses one floating-point Julian date, and the browser computes nothing but the text of its fields. `POST /api/astronomical-time/convert` reads an instant (`now`, a `calendar` in a timescale, a numeric offset, or an IANA zone, `jd` or `mjd` as decimal text in a named scale, `unix`, or a Julian or Besselian epoch in TT) and answers the UTC and local calendars, Unix milliseconds, each of the seven scales as calendar, JD, and MJD text, GMST and GAST, LMST and LAST with an observer, the differences between scales, the epochs, the day of the year, the signed hour angle LAST − RA of an apparent right ascension of date (J2000 is not accepted: LAST is referred to the true equinox of date), and `quality`. Angles are radians. ISO 8601 is a form of the tool that it sends as a calendar with an offset, and the tool checks the fields and the supported years (−4712 to 9999), so the server takes them as valid. The only errors are readings that name no single instant: `422 ambiguousLocalTime` with `{ earlier, later }` for a reading the clocks repeat, which the tool resolves with Earlier or Later (`fold`), and `422 nonexistentTime` for one they skip or a `:60` on a day without a leap second. An inserted leap second keeps its calendar and has no `unixMillis`, which POSIX cannot represent; on such a day JD(UTC) is the ERFA quasi-JD of a stretched day. Calendars are proleptic Gregorian with astronomical year numbering. Before 1960 UTC did not exist, so TAI−UTC is taken as 0 (`leapSeconds: beforeUtc`), and after the server's now no further leap second is assumed (`assumed`).
- Earth orientation (`EarthOrientationData`): the IERS tables of the whole server. `app/src/main.ts` calls `startEarthOrientation` before it serves, which reads them from `iers/` of the data directory (`DEFAULT_IERS_DIRECTORY`, under `NEBULOSA_HOME`) and installs them as the default providers of the toolkit (`TIME_PROVIDERS.dut1` and `.pm`, `installEarthOrientation`), so the Sky, the mounts, the pointing models, and the converter read one UT1 and one polar motion, and a refresh reaches them all. `iersab`, the shared table of the toolkit, stays empty, since its lookups clamp to the edge of a table, and a process that never starts them, such as a test, keeps UT1 = UTC and no polar motion. Of the workers only the fit worker needs them, and it reads the same cache, read only and once per worker, when the server thread has them (`earthOrientation` of its request): the hour angles of a fit then match those of the corrections. Solar eclipse maps use the toolkit's ΔT model and do not change with UT1, and streak classification does not read it. Saved pointing models are not refitted: UT1 only moves the hour angle of their basis, so terms of 1000″ move a correction by less than 0.1″ at the largest DUT1. Starting the server and any conversion finding the tables older than 7 days start one background download of finals2000A (IERS, then its USNO mirror) and EOP C04, at most once an hour, with no timer and no request waiting on it; a download replaces a table only if the new one reaches today (IERS A) or 120 days ago (IERS B). An instant no table holds takes DUT1 = 0 rather than the toolkit's clamped edge, leaves `dut1` and `ttMinusUt1` out, and says so in `quality.eop` (`covered`, `outsideTable`, or `notLoaded`), and the tool states UT1 and sidereal times as approximate. No observed or predicted label is given, since the tables do not keep it per row.
- Astronomical Time Converter tool: its state is a tab-lived valtio proxy (`astronomicalTimeState`), never persisted. Now converts the real current time every second while the page is visible, one request at a time, and never the Sky simulation clock; Custom sends an entry 250 ms after the last edit, superseding the request before it, and an entry the tool cannot read or a refused one leaves no result, so an old value never stands for new inputs. Changing form rewrites the entry for the instant shown. The location is a tab-memory `ToolLocation` (`ToolLocationSection`): the active profile's observing site, another saved site, or coordinates typed for the tool alone with their elevation and zone; the time zone is the one `resolveDisplayZone` gives for the tool's `DisplayZoneOverride` (`TimeZoneSelect`) and its location, always sent as an IANA name, since the server reads calendar entries and the local civil time in it. An Earlier or Later choice holds only for the zone it was made in. Use Sky Time copies the Sky's instant once and Set Sky Time sends the instant shown to `skyClock`, disabled inside a leap second; neither changes a device, the profile, or the status bar. Use Sky Selection copies the apparent right ascension of date of the selection, computed for its own instant, which the tool shows. Each copied value names its scale and is independent of the locale.
- Eclipse maps: computed in `api` from `core/src/astronomy/events/eclipse/*`, which produce geographic geometry and SVG paths, drawn on `WorldMap` (see World Map Contract). No map library is added.
- Eclipse ephemeris (`api/src/eclipses.ts`): the toolkit's series find an eclipse and `refineSolarEclipse` and `refineLunarEclipse` fix it, so the id, the type, and the times of a response are those of the refined eclipse. `eclipseSunMoon` gives every computation of one eclipse the same provider, `eraMoon98` corrected with three ELPMPP02 evaluations (`sunMoonPositionAround`), cached per day of the eclipse. `findRefinedEclipse` steps past an instant by the refined time, not the series' one, and starts 10 minutes before it, since a refined instant moves by up to about a minute. The lunar series also takes candidates up to 0.02 below magnitude 0 for the refinement to decide, which is what finds the faintest penumbral eclipses. Measured against the NASA catalogs for 2001–2100: solar greatest eclipse within 26 s and gamma within 0.0001; lunar greatest eclipse within 3 s, magnitude within 0.0003, and types all equal. The solar residual is not explained, and NASA's series differs from ELPMPP02. Local solar contacts are within 5 s of a DE421 oracle, with the smooth limb and NASA's k2 = 0.272281.
- Lunar eclipses (`api/src/lunareclipses.ts`): stateless, read-only queries, never operations or events. The id, the degree plane, and the location parameters are the eclipse conventions of `api/src/eclipses.ts`. An eclipse is named by its id, the Unix milliseconds of its greatest eclipse, resolved again on every request (an id that names no eclipse is `404 notFound`), so nothing is stored per client. `GET /api/eclipses/lunar` steps to the next or previous eclipse from an instant, strictly, so stepping from an id never returns the same eclipse; with a place, it skips eclipses whose Moon stays below its horizon, trying 24 eclipses at most (`MAX_VISIBLE_SCAN`, `404 notFound` beyond) and letting the event loop run between them. `/:id/map` answers the contact times, sublunar points, and per-contact moonrise/moonset lines and above-horizon regions as SVG path data on a degree plane (x = longitude + 180, y = 90 − latitude); `/:id/local` answers the circumstances of a place (visibility over the whole eclipse, contact altitudes, azimuths, P and Z angles, magnitudes, durations) and the Local View's shapes. Times are Unix milliseconds (UTC, converted from the toolkit's TT), angles degrees, durations seconds; contacts an eclipse lacks are absent. Visibility is the Moon's center at or above the geometric 0° horizon without refraction, on the map and locally alike. Whole-eclipse visibility zones on the map are not provided: the map shows visibility at one contact.
- Solar eclipses (`api/src/solareclipses.ts`, `solareclipses.map.ts`): stateless, read-only queries with the lunar conventions of `api/src/eclipses.ts` (id, strict stepping, `404 notFound`), never operations or events. `GET /api/eclipses/solar` steps to the next or previous eclipse; with a place, it skips eclipses whose Sun stays below its horizon during the local eclipse, trying 48 eclipses at most (`404 notFound` beyond) and letting the event loop run between them. `/:id/map` answers the named global points (P1–P4, U1–U4, C1/C2 the ends of the central line, MAX, N1/N2/S1/S2) with their times and the Sun's geometric altitude there, the greatest eclipse and greatest duration, and SVG path data on the degree plane for the total and annular parts of the central line (split where a hybrid eclipse changes), the northern and southern limits of the central and partial eclipse, and the sunrise/sunset curves; separate branches stay separate subpaths. A map costs hundreds of milliseconds, so it is computed on a worker of its own (`api/src/solareclipses.worker.ts`, embedded by `app/build.ts`), one at a time, with concurrent requests for one id sharing a job, a 30 s timeout (`500 computationFailed`), and the latest 8 maps cached. `/:id/local` answers the circumstances of a place computed in the API process with the same ephemeris as the map's Besselian elements: visibility (kind, central phase, Sun rising or setting, completeness of the contacts), C1, C2, MAX, C3, C4 with the Sun's altitude, P and Z, and magnitude, the maximum magnitude, Moon/Sun diameter ratio, partial and central durations, the width of the path here (`pathWidthKm`, the limits across the central line over the passage, as the catalogs publish it, for a place in the central path), the central shadow's chord through the place (`shadowPathWidthKm`, the narrowest width of its footprint at the local maximum, which can be several times smaller at a low Sun), and the Local View's shapes. Times are Unix milliseconds (UTC, converted from the toolkit's TT), angles degrees, durations seconds, widths kilometers; values an eclipse or a place lacks are absent. Locally, visible means the Sun's center at or above the 0° horizon, without the site's elevation, terrain, or the lunar limb profile; the map's limits use the toolkit's empirical horizon refraction, matching the NASA maps. Obscuration zones and animation of the shadow are not provided.
- Lunar Eclipse Map (`web/src/tools/lunareclipse/`): its state is a tab-lived valtio proxy (`lunarEclipseState`), never persisted, so leaving and reopening the tool keeps the eclipse, the place, and the presentation. It opens on the next eclipse after the real current time, never the Sky simulation clock, unless its URL names an eclipse, and observes from its own `ToolLocation`: the active profile's observing site until another saved site, coordinates typed in, or a click on the map is chosen, for the tool only, never the profile; a click gives coordinates at 0 m in the zone of the coordinates before, or UTC after a site. Only a new longitude or latitude asks for the local circumstances again; a click asks only for them; choosing a contact or a Local View presentation asks only for them again, and zooming or panning asks for nothing. Stale answers are discarded through request guards. Times show the date and the zone's name, in the zone `resolveDisplayZone` gives for the tool's `DisplayZoneOverride` (tab memory) and its location, whose change asks for nothing. The location and zone sections (`EclipseLocationSection`, `EclipseZoneSelect`), the map click (`mapLocation`), the time formatting, and the map's heading and legend are shared by the eclipse tools in `web/src/tools/eclipse/`; below the sm breakpoint the heading puts the eclipse type and date on two lines and the legend starts collapsed to a button. A URL that names an eclipse (`/tools/lunar-eclipse-map/<eclipse id>`, the id of `/api/eclipses/lunar`) shows that eclipse with its map (`showLunarEclipse`), with the same rules as the Solar Eclipse Map's link: shown whatever the tab showed before, on a link, a reload, back, or forward; an id that names no eclipse shows the error; stepping replaces the id in the URL.
- Solar Eclipse Map (`web/src/tools/solareclipse/`): built like the Lunar Eclipse Map, with a tab-lived valtio proxy (`solarEclipseState`), the shared place and time zone rules, and request guards. Its global map does not depend on a contact or a place, so a click or a contact asks only for the local circumstances again, and showing or hiding a layer (central line, central limits, partial limits, sunrise/sunset curves, points, point names; all shown at first) asks for nothing. The central line is solid where total and dashed where annular, so a hybrid eclipse reads without color. Every global point is drawn with its name (a layer of its own), with a marker shape per family, and the names of points that nearly coincide are stacked. A global point is chosen in the panel's Global Points list, since a click on the map chooses the place; the map then rings it and centers on it, and the panel tells what it is, where and when, the Sun's altitude, and for the greatest eclipse and duration the Sun's azimuth, the central phase, its duration, and the path width, and can make it the place. The Local Circumstances show the path width at the greatest eclipse, the path width here, and the shadow chord here under their own names. Global points are named apart from the local contacts: C1 and C2 on the map are the ends of the central path, never the contacts of a place. Contacts, durations, and the shadow chord a place lacks are left out, and the magnitude is a fraction of the solar diameter, never an obscured area. A URL that names an eclipse (`/tools/solar-eclipse-map/<eclipse id>`, the id of `/api/eclipses/solar`) shows that eclipse with its map (`showEclipse`, under the same request guard as the searches), whatever the tab showed before, on a link, a reload, back, or forward; an id that names no eclipse shows the error, never another eclipse. Stepping to another eclipse from such a URL replaces it with the new id, so the URL never names an eclipse other than the one shown; without an id the tool keeps its own choice.
- Close approaches (`api/src/closeapproaches.ts`): stateless, read-only queries of JPL, never operations or events, through the queue of the Sky Map Contract. The toolkit builds the requests and reads the tables (`closeApproachesUrl`, `parseCloseApproaches`); no list is computed or kept locally, so without JPL there is none. A row is an encounter, not an object: `GET /api/close-approaches` answers a page of CAD with its `total`, which CAD filters, sorts, and pages itself, and the same object appears once per encounter. `neoOnly` false lifts the NEO filter CAD applies by default; it does not select what is not a NEO. Observed behavior CAD does not document or documents otherwise: dates filter in TDB, a bare `date-max` means 00:00 (so the last day is sent as `T23:59:59`), `limit-from` is an offset (omitted on the first page), and `dist-max` also applies to one object, whose history is asked with 100 au. The NEO filter is refused for one object, so it is not sent. A page past the end has no rows and keeps its `total`. The time of a row is converted from the published Julian date TDB, whose text is kept with the TDB calendar date, through the toolkit's timescales, with leap seconds; before 1960 TAI − UTC is taken as 0, and in the future no further leap second is assumed. Distances are AU, with the 3σ bounds of CAD, the time uncertainty is the 3σ text of CAD, and the diameter uncertainty 1σ; a value CAD does not publish is absent, and no diameter is estimated from H. `GET /api/close-approaches/objects?designation=` resolves a row to its body by exact designation (SBDB `des`), since a row has no SPK-ID, and answers `found` with `MinorBody` and `MinorBodyDetails` (the published text of the orbit, physical parameters, discovery, radar, satellites, alternate orbits, and virtual impactors, without SBDB's embedded close approaches), `ambiguous`, or `notFound`, all with status 200. It shares the cache of `MinorBodies`, fresh for an hour here while the Sky keeps a body as long as it stays cached. `GET /api/close-approaches/objects/:spkId/approaches` pages the encounters of one body with CAD's `spk` selector. Pages stay cached for 5 minutes (64 pages, successes only) and `refresh=true` reads one again. The tool checks the filters, so the routes take them as valid; `400` from CAD is `502 rejected`, another major format `502 upstreamSchemaChanged`, an unreadable table `502 invalidResponse`, a 429, a 503, or a paused queue `503 rateLimited` with `retryAfter` in milliseconds, and any other failure `503 serviceUnavailable`. Virtual impactors are Sentry's as published through SBDB, an empty list says only that SBDB lists none, and nothing here computes or infers an impact probability.
- Close Approaches tool (`web/src/tools/closeapproaches/`): its state is a tab-lived valtio proxy (`closeApproachesState`), so leaving and reopening the tool keeps the search, the page, and the selection, and the first opening of a tab searches the next 30 days for the target and largest distance of the last search that ran (the profile's `closeApproaches` preferences, Earth within 10 LD by default; see Persistence and State), without the NEO filter, so comets are not left out. The filters hold pending values until Search, as every screen does, and the tool checks them, the 366-day budget of a search included, so a longer time is explored in steps; Refresh reads the page shown from JPL again and keeps its rows until the answer. Sorting and paging ask the server, from page 1 when the order changes, and rows are never sorted or sliced in the browser. The list, the object, and its history are independent requests, each behind a request guard, so an old answer never replaces a newer one, and no request ever repeats by itself: a failure shows why with Retry. A new search forgets the selection; another page keeps it, and another encounter of the same object reads nothing again. A failure of the object leaves the encounter usable. The dates of the filters are labeled TDB, the times of the rows are in the zone `resolveDisplayZone` gives for the tool's `DisplayZoneOverride` (`TimeZoneSelect`, the profile's default unless chosen) and the active site, always named in the header and, with why it applies, in the inspector, and the TDB of an encounter stays in its row title and in the inspector. The zone of the times and the distance unit (LD, AU, or km) are chosen above the table and kept in the same preferences; the unit never mixes within a value and its range; LD is the 384,400 km of JPL, a mean scale. On wide screens the inspector is the contextual panel; below `lg` it takes the place of the table, with Back to Results, since the shell keeps the state of its drawer to itself. Its sections are collapsible (`closeapproaches.*`), each shown only with something to show: Overview, Close Approach, Orbit, Physical Properties, Observations & Discovery, Virtual Impactors, and Other Approaches, paged by JPL by SPK-ID over a span and a target of its own. An unknown value is left out, and every term easily read as a risk (NEO, PHA, MOID, the 3σ range) says what it means; nothing is shown as a hazard. Open in Sky selects the body through `selectSkyObject` and leaves the clock alone; View in Sky at Approach Time first sets `skyClock` to the UTC instant of the encounter, since the selection reads it. Neither changes the observer or any device. Observability is read only while its section is open, through `POST /api/sky/position` with no endpoint of its own, at the encounter by default, the real current time, or the instant the Sky shows, and for the active profile's observing site, read again only when its place changes (`locationKey`), without which it is geocentric and offers Set Location. It states its source and fallback reason. Rise, transit, and set come from the local two-body orbit and the Horizons table is interpolated every 10 minutes, so during a very close flyby they lose accuracy, which the section says.
- Artificial Satellites tool (`web/src/tools/artificialsatellites/`, `/tools/artificial-satellites[/<noradId>]`): a tab-lived valtio proxy (`artificialSatellitesState`), never persisted, with the views Catalog, Passes, Ephemeris, and Ground Track over the routes of the Artificial Satellites Contract; it commands no device. Catalog searches the server's catalog only (text, group, favorites of the profile by id) and pages by 50. The workspace actions hold Import Elements, a popover that imports OMM or TLE text (kept in the tab's state when it closes), and Settings, a modal listing the CelesTrak groups with their state, an Automatic checkbox (the server's choice, shared by every profile), and a Download or Refresh that the server refuses inside its interval with its reason (a disabled button says until when). Every prediction is for the observing site of the active profile, with its elevation, and times are shown in the profile's display zone; the tool has no place or zone of its own. Without a site positions are geocentric and passes cannot be asked for, and Set Location opens Settings → Observing Sites. The Position Now of the panel is read again every `POSITION_REFRESH_MS` (15 s, the Sky's real-time tick) while a satellite with elements is selected and the page is shown, with RA and Dec formatted as the Sky does (`web/src/sky/format.ts`), as are those of the ephemeris table. Passes ask a window of 1 to 7 days from now or from the Sky's time, a minimum altitude, and a twilight limit, list each pass with its start (or "in progress"), highest point, end (or "continues"), duration, range, and conditions (visible, daylight, or the shadow), and draw the one selected as altitude against time through its three known points, its visible parts hatched and labeled. Ephemeris samples up to 72 hours (at most 5 000 rows, checked before sending) and exports the rows received as CSV with the units in the header, each time in UTC and in the display zone, whose IANA name is a column of its own (`time_civil`, `time_zone`). Ground Track (`SatelliteGroundTrack.tsx`, `groundtrack.ts`) draws on `WorldMap` the subpoint from one orbit before an instant (now or the Sky's time) to 1 to 5 orbits after it, dashed before the instant shown and solid after it, fainter in the penumbra and fainter still in the umbra, cut at the antimeridian (`splitAtAntimeridian`), with the satellite, its footprint (`smallCircleRegionPath`, a spherical Earth with an ideal horizon), the night side (`horizonRegionPath` of the subsolar point), and the observing site with whether it sees the satellite above its horizon; the footprint, night, observer, and shadow are layers of the legend, and hiding one asks for nothing. Points between samples are interpolated on great circles (`interpolateTrack` of the Sky), never predicted again. It is read when the view shows a satellite with elements; a ground track of now follows the real time on the 15 s tick and is read again once the satellite has gone an orbit past its instant, while one of the Sky's time stays put. A cursor chooses another instant of the window (Live returns to the real time), with its subpoint, height, illumination, and quality, and Show in Sky sets `skyClock` to it and selects the satellite there. It needs no observer, so another observer keeps it. Each kind of answer has a request guard, another satellite or another place of the site (`locationKey`, never its name or zone) drops the predictions of the previous one at once (`followSatelliteObserver`), and a change of the catalog (`satellites:changed`) reads the search, details, position, and a ground track shown again. Favorites, the element set used, and Open in Sky (with View in Sky at the Highest Point moving `skyClock` first) are explicit actions.
- Mount Remote Control (`api/src/mountremotecontrol.ts`, `web/src/tools/mountremotecontrol/`): exposes the MOUNT of the active profile's Equipment Setup to planetarium programs through `Lx200ProtocolServer` and `StellariumProtocolServer`. The tool never chooses a mount: without a MOUNT, or with one that is unavailable, disconnected, or switching, Start is disabled with the reason, and Equipment Setup is offered. A listener exists per (open connection id, exact mount id, protocol), so one mount may run an LX200 and a Stellarium listener at once and several mounts may be exposed by different tabs; the registry is process-wide, never per tab or profile, and switching profile, setup, or workspace, or closing the tab, never stops or redirects one. `POST …/remote-control/:protocol/start` with `{ host, port }` answers the listener (`MountRemoteServer`); a repeated start with the same binding answers it again, another binding is `409 conflict`, a taken address `409 addressInUse`, another bind failure `500 bindFailed`, and a failed start leaves the other listeners running. `/stop` (idempotent, 204) closes its clients and waits for their manual motions to stop; `DELETE …/clients/:clientId` closes one client (204, also when it already left). A listener stops with its mount (`device:updated` disconnected, `device:removed`) or connection (`connection:close`) and with the process, and is never restarted by itself. `GET /api/mount-remote-control` (`MountRemoteSnapshot`) and `mountRemoteControl:updated` carry an epoch per process and a revision per change, so the mirror (`listeners.ts`, in `SERVER_STORES`) drops a snapshot or start response older than an event it applied. Each client is shown as IP:port with Disconnect, nothing else. Clients are not authenticated; the default binding is `0.0.0.0` (every interface) on 10002 for LX200 and 10001 for Stellarium, and the tool warns whenever the host is not a loopback address. Commands run through the shared `MountCommands` commander and the operation registry, never a second command path: positions are the mount's (equinox of date) converted to J2000 at the server's time; a goto (`goToNow`, the same refusals as the goto route, above the horizon of the site the mount reports) or a sync (`syncNow`) is admitted or refused at once, LX200 answering it (`:MS#` `0` or `1Slew not possible#`, `:CM#` `#` or `Sync failed#`) and Stellarium only logging a refusal, and an admitted goto (`mount.goto`) goes on if its client leaves. An LX200 client's manual motion is its own `mount.remoteMove` operation, its commands run in order, another client's or a nudge's motion refuses it, and it stops when every axis is released, the client disconnects, the listener stops, or after `REMOTE_MOTION_WATCHDOG` (30 s); `:Q#` is the emergency stop of the mount. An LX200 client may set the mount's site (`:St#`, `:Sg#`, such as the GPS position Stellarium Mobile sends): the latitude and longitude are admitted at once, refused (`0`) while the mount is disconnected or held by an operation, and written together through the commander (`mountLocation`) after 250 ms, keeping the elevation; clock commands never change the mount (setters answer `0`), and slew rates are ignored. Stellarium clients receive the position every second. The host and port a listener was last started with are remembered per saved connection, mount, and protocol in the profile; a running listener always shows its actual binding.
- Meridian Flip Monitor (`api/src/meridianflip.ts`, `web/src/tools/meridianflip/`, `/tools/meridian-flip-monitor`, icon `meridian`): shows the server's monitor of the MOUNT of the active profile's Equipment Setup (see Meridian Flip Monitor Contract), which the status bar also shows while it is enabled. The tool never chooses a mount: without a MOUNT, or with one that is unavailable, disconnected, or switching, Enable (a workspace action, beside Disable) is disabled with the reason, and Equipment Setup is offered. The mirror (`meridianFlipState` of `meridianflip.ts`, in `SERVER_STORES`) drops a snapshot or response older than an event it applied by epoch and revision. It keeps the recent transitions of each enablement, loaded from `GET …/transitions` and appended from `meridianFlip:transition`. Closing the tab, switching profile, setup, or workspace, or reloading never enables, disables, or redirects a monitor. The view shows:
    - the availability and the derived headline, the signed hour angle (`formatHourAngle`, "geometry only" without a forecast), the transit as a countdown interpolated from `sampledAt` with `useNow`, which shows "Stale" after 65 s without a publication, and the window instants in UTC;
    - the reported and the geometric pier side, the mechanical assessment, and the reason in words;
    - a timeline of the warning, transit, window, and deadline with now, and a legend naming each color (the approach, the preferred window, its closing minutes, outside them) and each mark (the transit, now);
    - the settings in minutes (the settle time in seconds), held as a draft clamped by `normalizeMeridianFlipSettings` to what the server accepts. Enable and Apply remember them in the profile per mount, and Apply sends them with `expectedRevision`, a conflict showing the running settings;
    - a read-only exposure preview (`assess-exposure`, with the current `contextId`);
    - the recent transitions, newest first.

    Nothing here commands the mount.

- Alpaca Server (planned: a catalog entry without a view, and no `api` service yet): an `api` service over `AlpacaServer` (`core/src/devices/alpaca/server.ts`), started and stopped explicitly, bound to localhost by default, publishing only an explicit allowlist of devices. Devices imported from Alpaca connections are never exported, to avoid publication loops. Its state is published as events. The Tools view only controls it.

## Astro Weather Contract

- Astro Weather (`api/src/astroweather.ts`, `astroweather.openmeteo.ts`, `astroweather.seventimer.ts`, `astroweather.night.ts`) is stateless and read-only, never an operation or an event, and nothing here reads or commands a device, opens a dome, or changes a sequence. It answers planning questions from forecasts and is not a safety system: a forecast is never presented as an observation.
- `GET /api/astro-weather/forecast?latitude&longitude` (`AstroWeatherForecast`) reads two fixed public services, no key needed, and no URL or service is ever taken from a request. Each is read and cached apart, so one failing leaves the other usable, with `AstroWeatherSource` telling its `state` (`fresh`, `stale`, `unavailable`), download time, first and last sample, model run when known, and `errorCode` (`upstreamUnavailable`, `invalidResponse`, `rateLimited`). Neither having anything to show is `502 upstreamUnavailable` with `sources`, never empty arrays that could read as good weather.
- Open-Meteo gives the hourly weather (`MeteorologySample`) for 8 UTC days, which covers the dawn after the seventh local evening in any zone, with the service's default `best_match` model, which names no run. Cloud, temperature, humidity, dew point, and sustained wind are instantaneous; precipitation, its probability, and gusts describe the hour before their sample. Upstream seconds become milliseconds once. The observer's elevation is not sent, since it is a height above the ellipsoid and the service expects terrain, so the forecast's own height is reported with the grid cell.
- 7Timer! ASTRO gives seeing and transparency (`AstronomicalSample`): classes 1 (best) to 8 (worst), read for those two only, as discrete samples 3 hours apart counted in hours from a model run in UTC, for about 3 days from a run that may be hours old, so coverage is always read from the samples. A class is the service's category for a band, seeing in arcseconds (below 0.5, 0.5–0.75, 0.75–1, 1–1.25, 1.25–1.5, 1.5–2, 2–2.5, above 2.5) and transparency in magnitudes per airmass (below 0.3, 0.3–0.4, 0.4–0.5, 0.5–0.6, 0.6–0.7, 0.7–0.85, 0.85–1, above 1), never a measurement of one value. `-9999` and anything outside 1 to 8 are unknown. Observed behavior the service does not document or documents otherwise: `api.pl` redirects to its `astro.php`, which rounds the place to 0.1° (`cellDegrees`), not 0.001°, and JSON arrives labeled `text/html`, so its content type is not checked. `ac` stays 0: its modes are 2 and 7 km corrections, not a site elevation.
- Through `RemoteCache` with its limits (`RemoteCacheOptions`): fresh for 30 minutes (Open-Meteo) or 2 hours (7Timer!), one download shared by concurrent requests, a 12 s timeout, a 1 MiB limit, 128 places per service keyed at 0.01° and 0.1°, a failure repeated for a minute without downloading, and the last good value answered as `stale` for up to 6 or 12 hours from its download. The cache is shared by every tab, so no route bypasses it. The free services allow fewer than 10,000 Open-Meteo calls a day, and 7Timer! states no limit.
- `POST /api/astro-weather/night` (`AstroWeatherNightRequest`, `AstroWeatherNight`) answers the night containing an instant, computed from the toolkit and never the network. A night runs from one upper transit of the Sun to the next (`sunNightAt`), so it is named by its evening's civil date, dawn needs no second day, and any zone, even UTC for a site far from Greenwich, gives every night one evening, which civil days do not. The previous night is asked with `start − 1` and the next with `end`. Crossings use the convention of the Sun Inspector, absent ones are absent, and `darkness` is the time the Sun's center stays below −18°, empty when it never does. `display` runs from an hour before sunset to an hour after sunrise, or to the end of the night missing one. The Moon's illuminated fraction and phase are taken at the middle of `display`, and its intervals above the horizon (`moonAboveHorizon`, the rise and set convention of `SkyEphemeris.events`, on a 15-minute search) cover `display`, however many rises and sets fall in it; a grazing pass near a polar latitude can be missed. This is site-wide guidance: nothing knows a target or its separation from the Moon. A night costs about half a second of local computation, so the latest 10 nights of 16 sites are kept and found by the instant they contain.
- Reading a forecast (`web/src/tools/astroweather/observing.ts`) is pure and deterministic, with no request and no score: every hour of `display` gets `favorable`, `variable`, `unfavorable`, or `unavailable`, with its reasons and what it misses, beside a reading of the weather alone. Its limits are planning heuristics in constants, not scientific or equipment limits. Instantaneous values come from the sample starting an hour, and precipitation and gusts from the one ending it. Seeing and transparency come from the nearest 7Timer! sample within 90 minutes, labeled as such, never interpolated or carried past the last sample. A known hazard stands whatever else is missing and nothing offsets it; otherwise an hour missing what its mode needs is `unavailable`, never favorable. Every mode needs the weather, Deep Sky transparency, and Planetary seeing, so Visual keeps its windows past the horizon of 7Timer!. Deep Sky uses astronomical darkness and reads a bright Moon (at least half lit, above the horizon) as `variable`, Visual observes between the civil twilights and only notes the Moon, and Planetary observes between sunset and sunrise and ignores the Moon, reading gusts and seeing instead. A best window is at least 2 continuous favorable hours, the longest first and then the one with fewer cautions, clipped to the part of the night the mode can use and never in the past. A stale forecast the mode depends on is shown and recommends nothing; with no window the tool says why, and may offer the longest variable stretch as marginal.
- Astro Weather tool (`web/src/tools/astroweather/`, `/tools/astro-weather`): its state is a tab-lived valtio proxy (`astroWeatherState`); only the observing mode is a profile preference (see Persistence and State). The location is a tab-memory `ToolLocation` chosen in the shared `ToolLocationSection`: the active profile's observing site, followed live, another saved site, or coordinates typed with their elevation and a zone (UTC until one is chosen); it never changes the profile, and another profile resets it. Nights are named in the zone of the location (the `timeZone` of `/night`), and their times are shown in the zone `resolveDisplayZone` gives for the tool's `DisplayZoneOverride` (tab memory, `TimeZoneSelect`), which only reformats: the forecast and the night are keyed by the place and the location's zone (`siteKey`), never by the display zone. Its answers are read behind one request guard each for the forecast and the night, so a failure of one leaves the other shown and a late answer for another site is discarded; another site clears both at once. Changing mode, night, or hour asks for no forecast, and only a new site, Refresh, or reopening after 30 minutes does, with the cache of the server deciding whether a service is read at all; nothing polls. It opens on the current night, the one in progress until its `display` ends and the next one from then on, even before the upper transit that starts it, moves to that next night by itself while the tool stays open, asking for no forecast, goes back no further, and goes forward while the forecast has samples for the next night. The page shows the site and its zone, **Forecast — not live observations**, the night and mode, the outlook with its decisive reasons first, the window, six facts of the part of the night the mode can use, and a notice per service that failed, is old, or ends during the night. The timeline (`ForecastTimeline.tsx`) has rows of the outlook, cloud, the two classes, rain chance, gusts, and the Moon on one time axis over bands of how dark the sky is, each row on its own scale. Hours are bars, 7Timer! samples are tiles centered on their timestamps and 3 hours wide, a missing value draws nothing, and an hour is chosen by a click or with the arrow keys, Home, and End, never by hovering; each reading has a symbol as well as a color. The contextual panel holds the location, the time zone, Sky Conditions and Weather Conditions of the hour chosen, Astronomical Conditions of the night (`astroWeather.*`), and Sources with the credits. Without a location the tool offers Set Location, which opens Settings → Observing Sites. Nothing here is passed to the Sky, the Sequencer, or a device.
- Both free services are for non-commercial use: Open-Meteo under CC BY 4.0, 7Timer! with no formal license and public pages that conflict about paid software. Commercial distribution stays blocked until that is settled.

## Artificial Satellites Contract

- Catalog (`api/src/satellites.store.ts`, see Persistence and State): objects by NORAD catalog number, always a canonical decimal string of any length (six- and nine-digit ids included, never a number or a five-digit pattern), with their SATCAT metadata when read, one GP and one imported element set each, and group memberships. A group is a membership of the source that listed the object, never a category, and an object is kept with its other groups, metadata, and elements when a group stops listing it. An object without elements is metadata only: searchable, never predicted. The element set the predictions use is the one an operator chose (`PUT /api/satellites/:noradId/element-source`), else GP, else imported, never the most recently downloaded. Search (`GET /api/satellites`) is local, never the network: an exact NORAD id (leading zeros ignored) or international designator first, then names starting with the text, then names containing it, case and diacritics ignored, filtered by group, SATCAT type, status, and a list of ids, paged by offset (at most `SATELLITE_SEARCH_LIMIT`, 100, per page; 50 by default) with its total. `GET /api/satellites/:noradId` answers the details of one object and `GET /api/satellites/sources` the state of every source.
- Element sets (`api/src/satellites.elements.ts`): every set, downloaded or imported, is validated once by the toolkit (`validateOMM`, `validateTLE`: frame, center, time system, theory, numbers, epoch, eccentricity, mean motion, SGP4 initialization) and stored as a canonical OMM (CCSDS keywords in a fixed order, numbers as numbers) whose SHA-256 is its identity; a set that fails is never stored and is counted with its reason. Imports (`POST /api/satellites/import`) take OMM JSON or TLE text (two lines, optionally after a name line, Alpha-5 numbers converted) and make the imported set the one the object uses; a text that is neither is `422 invalidImport`. Every prediction names its set (`elements`: digest, epoch, kind, source key), so two results from different sets are never confused.
- Downloads (`api/src/satellites.sync.ts`, `satellites.celestrak.ts`) follow the CelesTrak usage policy (https://celestrak.org/usage-policy.php): only when an operator asks (`POST /api/satellites/sources/:sourceKey/refresh`) or for the groups an operator turned on for automatic downloads, never when the Sky or the tool opens, a selection changes, or time is animated; only allowlisted queries (`gp` or `satcat`, `GROUP` of a fixed list of current-data groups or `CATNR`), always `FORMAT=JSON` from `https://celestrak.org`, with the Nebulosa `User-Agent` (`identifyCelestrakClient`) and redirects not followed; one request at a time for the process and one shared download per source. A source is downloaded at most once per GP update (2 hours) or SATCAT interval (24 hours); the attempt is recorded before the request, so a failure or a crash never leads to a repeat, and a refresh inside the interval is `409 sourceCooldown` with the cached data untouched. Any answer other than 200 or no answer blocks the source for its interval, doubled by each failure since the last success up to a day and at least the `Retry-After` asked for (`409 sourceBlocked` meanwhile); a 301, 403, 429, or 5xx blocks every source, since CelesTrak firewalls clients that keep sending errors. The interval and blocks are kept in `satellite_sources` across restarts. An answer that is not a JSON list, or a group that answers nothing while it listed objects, is `502 invalidResponse` and replaces nothing; a single object without GP data is an empty answer. A GP download of a group that is not large (not ACTIVE, STARLINK, or ONEWEB) also downloads the SATCAT of the group when its own interval allows, whose failure never fails the first. Each successful download is published in one transaction and then `satellites:changed` carries a new revision.
- Automatic downloads (`api/src/satellites.auto.ts`, `SatelliteAutoDownload`): the allowlisted groups turned on (`PUT /api/satellites/sources/:sourceKey/auto-download` with `{ enabled }`, the GP source key of a group only, else `404 notFound`; kept in `satellite_auto_downloads`, large groups included) are downloaded once when the server starts (`startSatelliteDownloads`, after the database) and then by an in-process `Bun.cron` job every 6 hours (`0 */6 * * *`, the server's time zone), one after the other through the same downloads as an operator's, so the 2-hour GP interval, the SATCAT interval, and the blocks hold: a group not allowed yet waits for a later run, and a failure is recorded in its source and never stops the other groups or the schedule. A run joins the one in progress; a hot reload of the server stops the job before the next run starts again. A large group downloads its elements only, as when asked. Turning a group on downloads nothing by itself and publishes `satellites:changed`.
- Predictions (`api/src/satellites.ephemeris.ts`) use only the toolkit's SGP4 and frames, from a prepared record per element digest, copied for every computation since SGP4 writes into a record as it propagates. Positions (`/position`, `/ephemeris`, `/tracks`) are geometric: no light time (a few milliseconds) and no annual aberration, which a satellite moving with the Earth does not show; topocentric for an observer and geocentric without one, labeled by `center`. RA/Dec are given of date (true equator and equinox, the frame of the Sky map) and in ICRS (J2000); altitude is geometric, with `apparentAltitude` refracted by the model of the Sky; distances are km, rates km/s and radians per second. Illumination is the toolkit's conical umbra and penumbra, with the Sun interpolated between hourly VSOP87E positions (0.01″). `/ephemeris` samples at most 5000 rows (`truncated` beyond them), letting the event loop run every 250 rows. Satellites have no rise, transit, and set events.
- Passes (`/passes`, the tool keeps the window within 7 days): the intervals above a minimum geometric altitude (`satelliteWindowPasses`), searched a day at a time with the event loop running between days and a pass cut by a day boundary joined again, at most 200. A pass in progress at an edge of the window is kept with its bound at the edge (`startsBeforeWindow`, `endsAfterWindow`), never shown as a rise or set, and a geostationary satellite above the mask is one pass over the whole window. The culmination is the highest point within the window, not a meridian transit or the closest approach. Each pass lists its visible parts: sunlit (outside the umbra) while the observer's Sun is below the twilight limit given; no brightness is estimated, since no source gives the standard magnitude of most objects, so brightness is unknown and never filters. A cancelled request stops computing.
- Quality of every prediction (`quality`): the age of the elements (instant minus epoch, in days, the worst over a window) against policy limits by orbit (below 225 minutes: caution after 3 days, unavailable after 14; higher: 14 and 60), the decay date of the catalog, an SGP4 failure, and whether the Earth orientation tables hold the instant; `usable`, `caution`, or `unavailable` with its reasons. These are conservative limits, not error bars. Passes are refused when unavailable (`422 predictionUnreliable` with `quality`); positions and ephemerides answer with their quality; an SGP4 failure is `422 predictionUnreliable`. A metadata-only object is `404 noElements`, an unknown one `404 notFound`.
- Ground tracks (`POST /api/satellites/:noradId/ground-track` with `{ time, orbits }`, `SatelliteGroundTrackResponse`): samples from one orbit before the instant to 1 to 5 orbits after it (`GROUND_TRACK_MAX_ORBITS`), at most 3 days in all (`GROUND_TRACK_MAX_MS`), every period/180 between 10 s and 5 minutes in whole seconds, at most 5000, in parallel arrays: the geodetic subpoint (`satelliteSubpoint`, IERS2010, longitude in (−π, π]) and height, the footprint as the geocentric half-angle of the region that sees the satellite above the horizon (`satelliteGroundFootprint`: a spherical Earth with an ideal horizon, without terrain, refraction, or a minimum altitude), the shadow state, and the subsolar point (the toolkit's `subpoint` of the interpolated VSOP87E Sun). It needs no observer, lets the event loop run every 250 samples, carries the elements and the quality of its window, and is never refused for the age of the elements; an SGP4 failure is `422 predictionUnreliable`.
- Tracks for the Sky (`POST /api/satellites/tracks`): samples of RA/Dec of date, geometric altitude, and sunlit state of the ids asked for and of the members of a group above the observer's horizon at the start (by the look angles alone, highest first; the first by id without an observer), at most 50 objects and 20 000 samples, each with its elements and quality or the code of its failure.
- Sky identity (`api/src/sky/`): a satellite is the `SkyObjectRef` `{ kind: 'satellite', noradId }` (key `satellite:<id>` in the web) of category `satellite`. `GET /api/sky/search` lists at most 5 satellites of the local catalog after the bodies and constellations, never asking the network; `POST /api/sky/position` locates a satellite through the satellite service (`SkySatellites`, `satelliteSkyAdapter`) as a `SkyPosition` of source `local` with the refracted altitude, the distance in AU, and no magnitude or events, `404 noElements` for a metadata-only one and `422 predictionUnreliable` when SGP4 fails. The Sky locator of the mounts has no satellite target, so no mount is ever sent to one.
- Sky map (`web/src/sky/satellites.sky.ts`, `satellites.track.ts`): the selected satellite and the profile's favorites, at most `SATELLITE_TRACKS_LIMIT` (50), are moving bodies of type `satellite` drawn at every zoom by the map layer of their own, `satellites` (`MovingBodyLayer` and `movingBodyLayerId` of `web/src/sky/celestial/`), which the display layer Artificial satellites shows or hides (`satellites` of `SKY_LAYERS`, shown by default) apart from the Solar system layer. Hidden, no satellite is drawn or sampled, the selected one and its track included. Their positions come from one `/tracks` request per window of 60 samples around the Sky clock's instant (about ten real minutes at the clock's rate, from 10 minutes to 6 hours, starting a tenth before the instant, aligned to its step), asked again only when the instant leaves the window or the ids, the observer, or the catalog revision change, and placed between samples by spherical linear interpolation, never a new prediction. The markers move every 250 ms (1 s with reduced motion) while the page is shown, apart from the 15 s tick of the map, since each move rebuilds the pick index.
- Satellite Inspector (`web/src/sky/satellite/`, `skySatelliteState`, tab memory): the Object Inspector of a satellite, with its identity, the Brightness Unknown, the position of the selection (geometric, of date and J2000, distance in km, geocentric without an observer), the altitude and azimuth, the next pass above the horizon within two days of the Sky's instant on request (Show Next Pass, visible parts with the Sun below civil twilight), and the orbital data. Track on Sky pans the view to the satellite at every marker update until the map is panned (the projection never moves, so a satellite outside the equatorial chart's hemisphere cannot be centred); Show Track draws the samples of its window as a dashed line, broken below the horizon; Open in Artificial Satellites opens the tool on it. No Slew, Framing, rise, transit, or set is offered.
- Favorites are a profile preference the server keeps (`ProfileSettings.satelliteFavorites`, see Persistence and State); a refresh never removes an id it stops listing. The web mirrors the sources (`satelliteCatalogState` of `web/src/satellites/satellites.ts`, in `SERVER_STORES`), whose revision the views watch to read their own data again after a change.
- Not provided: SupGP, the Sky display of a whole group (the API samples one), satellite brightness and flares, conjunction or collision screening, trails over images, transits of the Sun or the Moon, and tracking with a mount.

## External Tools Contract

- External programs and services the backend uses are registered in `api/src/externaltools.ts` with the capabilities their adapters provide and the settings they take besides a timeout (`fields`): ASTAP (plate solving and star detection, through `core/src/astrometry/solvers/astap.ts`; executable), Astrometry.net Local (`solve-field`; executable and an optional backend configuration file naming the index directories), and Nova Astrometry.net (the web service; endpoint, empty for the public one). The native `detectStars` and the libastrometry binding are not external tools: the binding is not integrated, because `solver_run` is one blocking native call that cannot be interrupted and whose crash would take the server down, and because it takes stars already detected, which would need a detection configuration of its own. No route runs an arbitrary command.
- A web service (`session: true`) is signed in to with the API key of an account: `POST /api/settings/external-tools/:id/login` sends the key once to the saved endpoint and keeps only the session the service returns, in the memory of the server, so a restart signs out; neither the key nor the session is ever stored, and the key is never logged. A refused key is 422 with the service's message, an unreachable service 502. `POST …/logout` forgets the session, and saving another endpoint signs out. Clients only see `signedIn`; the session itself reaches only the services through `resolve`. A web service is configured when signed in. Its Test checks the endpoint and the session the server holds without contacting the service, so an expired session is found by the next run.
- Settings belong to the server instance, never to a profile or a browser: Settings (the top bar gear button) → External Tools (`/settings/external-tools/<id>`) edits a draft, and only Save writes it (`PUT /api/settings/external-tools/:id`), with the revision it was edited from; a save from an outdated revision is refused with `409 conflict` and the current state, and every change is published as `externalTool:updated`. `GET /api/settings/external-tools` lists every program and `GET …/:id` one.
- With no executable saved (no session for a web service), a program is Not Configured and `ExternalToolsStore.resolve` returns undefined, so its services must refuse to run it; no platform default path is assumed. A service resolves the settings once when an operation starts and keeps that snapshot until the operation ends, so a save applies to the next operations without a restart.
- Test (`POST …/:id/test`) checks a draft on the server's filesystem without saving it: the path exists, is a regular file, and may be executed (on Windows any existing file passes); a bare name is looked up on the server's PATH; a configuration file, when set, must exist. It never starts the program, so it proves neither that the program runs nor that it analyzes images, and the version is reported as unknown. Only a check of the saved settings becomes the program's latest check, kept in memory until the server restarts.
- There is no authentication: any client that can reach the API can change the executable the server runs and check paths of its filesystem, like `/api/files` lists them. Keep the host the server listens on (`--host`, else `APP_HOST`; `app/src/address.ts`) on localhost when untrusted clients can reach the network.
- Settings are rows of `external_tool_settings` in the operational database, one per program saved at least once; a program without a row runs with the defaults.

## Star Detection Contract

- One service, `StarDetectionService` (`api/src/stars.ts`, analysis jobs of the Shared Image Contracts with routes `/api/images/:id/stars` and `/api/stars`, event `stars:updated`), detects the stars of an image document for Images → Star Detection (manual runs) and Auto Focus. Captures never analyze their frames; Guiding, the stacking analyses, and the Star Profile Inspector run the toolkit's detector on their own. Results are the toolkit's `DetectedStar` (x, y, HFD, SNR, flux) in 0-based pixel coordinates of the document, origin at the upper-left sample, +X right, +Y down.
- Providers: `builtin` runs `detectStars` on a worker of its own (`api/src/stars.worker.ts`), fed the grayscale copy of `ImageStore.detectionImage` (a CFA mosaic debayered with its trusted pattern first), one at a time, cancelled or stopped after 120 s (`timeout`) by terminating its worker. `astap` runs `astapDetectStars` on a FITS copy in a per-job temporary directory, one process at a time, killed through its signal, with the External Tools settings resolved once per job (or per Auto Focus session); a submission while ASTAP is not configured is `422 astapUnavailable`, and its failures are `astapUnavailable`, `astapTimeout`, or `astapFailed`. A failure never falls back to the other provider. An equivalent request is the same image, detector settings, acceptance, and ASTAP executable; only its kept result is reused, never a job still waiting or running.
- Accepted stars have finite coordinates inside the image less an edge margin, a finite positive HFD within the limits, and finite positive SNR (at least the minimum) and flux. The aggregate HFD is the median HFD of the accepted stars, in pixels, and is unavailable (never 0) below 3 accepted stars. HFDs of the two providers are computed differently and must not be compared.
- Image quality (`web/src/stars/quality.ts`, `ImageQualitySummary`) is derived from the latest star detection known for an image id, whoever asked for it, so the HFD an Auto Focus measured on a frame is shown with that frame without detecting again. Its states are `not-measured` (no detection, or the latest was cancelled or skipped), `pending`, `measured` (an HFD only when enough stars were accepted), and `failed`; absence is never zero, and the summary keeps the provider and request an HFD is only comparable under. The Image Inspector shows it in its Quality section; nothing is measured automatically, and per-frame quality history is not kept yet.
- The request (detector, its settings, acceptance) is one profile preference, `ProfileSettings.starDetection` (`starDetectionSettings`, `setStarDetectionSettings` in `web/src/profile/profile.ts`), edited in Images → Star Detection (also before the document has an image, as a camera tab waiting for its first frame, with Run Detection disabled until it has one) and shared by every document and by the frames of Auto Focus and Guiding; a document keeps only how its markers are shown. The built-in detector's "Aperture radius" (`maximumRadius`) defaults to 32 px (`DEFAULT_APERTURE_RADIUS` of `web/src/stars/stars.ts`), and the web client always sends it; the aperture grows only while a star extends beyond it, so focused stars measure as with the toolkit's fixed 4 px one.
- Auto Focus integration: `StarDetectionService.measure(imageId, request, signal, origin)` resolves to the result of that image, whatever newer frame arrives, and cancels with its signal; it also takes a preparation resolved once, so a session keeps its ASTAP settings. Auto Focus jobs carry the `autofocus` origin.
- Limitations: a request without `maximumRadius` measures the built-in HFD inside the fixed 4 px aperture, which saturates near 8 px for defocused stars; the built-in detector returns at most 2000 stars (`BUILTIN_STAR_LIMIT`); saturation is not detected; a frame replaced on screen before its result is fetched shows no markers.

## Star Profile Inspector Contract

- `POST /api/images/:id/star-profile` (`createStarProfileRoutes`, `api/src/starprofile.ts`) measures the star a person pointed at in an image document, for as long as the request lasts. It is a short, explicit interaction, so unlike the analyses of `analysis.ts` it has no job, event, snapshot, or kept result, and holds no device. Its body is a `StarProfileRequest`: the point (0-based pixel, integer centres), the `model`, `searchRadius`, `maximumRadius`, `minSNR`, and `detected` when a star detection already found a star there.
- It answers 200 with a `StarProfileResult`: `measured`, even when the star is degraded or invalid, as its `flags`, `valid`, and `quality` say, or `noStar` with the reason `noPeak` or `ambiguous`. A closed image is 404, an aborted request 409 `superseded`, too many requests waiting 503 `queueFull`, and a failed measurement 500 `workerFailed` or `timeout`.
- Measurements run one at a time on a worker of their own (`api/src/starprofile.worker.ts`), at most 8 waiting, in arrival order. A request retains its image from the start. It reads only a region around the point (`ImageStore.detectionRegion`, see Shared Image Contracts), reaching `2 × searchRadius + maximumRadius + 4` pixels on every side, so a click never copies the frame and a region edge never flags a star: `nearBorder` and `clipped` only come from the frame edges. An abort takes a request out of the queue; one on the worker cannot be interrupted, so it finishes, its result is dropped, and no other request of the same image is touched. A worker that dies or runs for 10 s is replaced.
- The toolkit does the science (`inspectStarRegion`, `api/src/starprofile.analysis.ts`): `detectStars` finds the star nearest the point within `searchRadius`, without the score-gap trim, which is meant for a whole frame, and `measureStarProfile` measures it, sizing its aperture up to `maximumRadius`. The brightest sample of the region is never taken as the star. Two stars less than 1 px from being equally near are `ambiguous`, and so is a centroid that moved more than `searchRadius` from its candidate, which followed something else. `detectStars` cannot look within 8 px of a frame edge, so with no candidate there the point itself is measured, and kept only with an HFD of at least 1 px and a peak of at least 6 deviations; the SNR of the profile proves nothing here, since it counts positive flux only and grows with the aperture on a bare sky.
- Images → Star Profile Inspector (`web/src/images/StarProfileInspectorPanel.tsx`, store `web/src/stars/starprofile.ts`) is an image tool of every document. Selecting it runs nothing, and neither does editing its settings: only a click on the image (`onImageClick`, after Star Detection in the click owners) or Analyze Star on the star selected. A click on a star of a loaded star detection sends that star as `detected`; a click off the image is ignored. Hiding the overlays stops the clicks without touching the star, and on a camera tab a click holds the frame (`holdCameraFrame`).
- The latest measurement lives in `ImageViewState.starProfile`, tab memory only, bound to the image id it measured: `starProfileOf` never returns it for another image. A newer measurement aborts the one before it, an answer commits only under its own token, and `clearStarProfile` aborts and drops it when the document closes or the transaction of a camera tab shows another frame. Requests of other tabs are their own. The marker (`web/src/stars/StarProfileOverlay.tsx`) is drawn while the tool is open, in image pixels. The preview (`web/src/stars/StarPreview.tsx`) draws the samples of the response from the sky to the peak, never the rendering of the document. The plot (`web/src/stars/StarProfilePlot.tsx`) and the texts of a profile are shared with the Guiding panel. Widths show arcseconds only under a solution of the same image whose scale is the same along both axes (`isotropicPixelScaleAt`).
- The settings (model, search radius, aperture radius, minimum SNR, and what the preview draws) are one profile preference, `ProfileSettings.starProfile` (`starProfileSettings`, `setStarProfileSettings`), shared by every document. A measured star is never stored.
- `requestedModel` is what was asked and `profile.model` what was delivered: a rejected Moffat fit falls back to the moments, with `moffat.reason` and the `poorFit` flag. FWHM is Gaussian-equivalent for the moments and fitted for Moffat; neither is the half-maximum width of a cut. The HFD is the curve-of-growth one of the toolkit under either model, and it is not comparable with a `DetectedStar` HFD.
- Positions are pixels of the image, centroids included. The cuts go through the pixel nearest the centroid, with `offsets` fractional from the centroid and `values` above the background of the profile; `model` is the accepted fit at those same pixels (`moffatProfileValue`) and is absent without one. Cuts and preview reach `2 × HFD` pixels, from 12 (cuts) or 15 (preview) up to 32. Intensities are normalized samples, and `conversion` (`SampleConversion`) turns them into the scale of the source.
- Limitations: a star whose aperture holds a much brighter neighbor may answer `ambiguous`, since the moments follow the neighbor. A Moffat fit is often rejected on a faint star, whose residual is mostly noise, or a nearly Gaussian one, and the moments stand for it. Saturation is judged against the full scale: it is unknown on an image the read rescaled (`saturationKnown` false), and a debayered mosaic averages it away at the edge of a star. The SNR is the background-limited estimate of the toolkit, not a photon-noise model. A mosaic without a trusted pattern is measured as is.

## Streak Detection Contract

- One service, `StreakDetectionService` (`api/src/streaks.ts`, analysis jobs of the Shared Image Contracts with routes `/api/images/:id/streaks` and `/api/streaks` and event `streaks:updated`), detects the approximately straight streaks of an image document for Images → Streak Detection (manual runs only; no capture, sequence, or other analysis starts one). It runs the toolkit's `detectStreaks` on a worker of its own (`api/src/streaks.worker.ts`), fed `ImageStore.processingImage`: a full copy of the decoded samples in their own precision and layout, mono, RGB, or a CFA mosaic with its trusted pattern, never the debayered luminance copy of Star Detection nor anything the viewer applies (debayering, stretch, inversion, zoom). The analysis plane is `auto` (mono, RGB green, CFA green1) unless the settings name one the image has. Bright pixels whose gradient coherence is below `minCoherence` (stars, hot pixels) neither vote nor count in the photometry, so a trail crossing a crowded field is found; 0 keeps them, which a dotted trail of dashes shorter than about 7 px needs. The width is measured from the profile core on the fitted axis, and a line whose corridor holds a brighter structure beside the axis is rejected; a streak supported over less than `minCoverage` (0.5) of its length is rejected too, so a dashed trail needs a lower value.
- A `Streak` is in 0-based pixel coordinates of the received image (origin at the upper-left sample, +X right, +Y down), even on a CFA plane; its axial angle is in radians in [0, π), shown in degrees. The whole image is analyzed (no ROI), and the saturation level is never sent: the toolkit compares it with normalized samples. Photometry is in normalized sample units; an absent SNR or saturation fraction stays absent, never 0.
- The worker then classifies the streaks with `classifyStreaks`, from evidence of the same image only: its samples, its exposure and DATE-OBS, its WCS, and the accepted stars of a star detection. The WCS is the solution of the completed plate solving job of the image the request names (`plateSolveJobId`), else the valid WCS of its header; the stars are those of the completed star detection job of the image the request names (`starDetectionJobId`). The service resolves them through `PlateSolvingService.result`/`solutions` and `StarDetectionService.result` when the job is submitted and freezes them, so a later solve or detection never changes a job; a named job unknown, no longer kept, of another image, or without a solution is refused (422 `contextUnavailable`). Nothing is solved or detected for the classification, and no ephemeris, radiant, tracking quality, prior frame, network, or catalog is used. Each job publishes the sources it used (`context`).
- A classification is the toolkit's uncalibrated evidence weight: `unknown` unless the evidence singles out a class, and never shown as a probability. It is kept apart from the detector's `confidence`, by index in `classifications`. A classification that throws leaves the streaks unclassified (`classified: false`, a warning) instead of failing the job.
- One detection runs at a time. An equivalent request (image, every detector setting, context job ids) is answered with the job still waiting or running (202), or with its kept result. A running detection is cancelled, or stopped after 120 s (`timeout`), by terminating its worker. The settings are not validated by the API: the toolkit refuses invalid ones or a job beyond its bounded Hough work, and the job fails as `detectionFailed` with its reason, while a job beyond its bounded refinement work completes with the streaks refined so far; the web form keeps every setting within the toolkit's ranges and the orientation tolerance within 16 steps of the angle step.
- The detector settings are one profile preference, `ProfileSettings.streakDetection` (`streakDetectionSettings`, `setStreakDetectionSettings`), shared by every document and editable before a camera tab has a frame. Whether the next detection uses the plate solution shown and the latest stars of the image, how the lines are drawn, and the selected streak belong to the document view (`ImageStreaksState`). Run Detection holds the frame a camera tab shows. The lines (`web/src/streaks/StreakOverlay.tsx`) are drawn only over the image their result measured, without arrows; a click on a line (within 8 screen pixels) or a row of the list selects the same streak, and the selection of a camera tab is cleared when another frame is committed.
- Limitations: chance alignments of stars in a very crowded field can be reported as streaks, more often with a small max width, and a trail among an extreme density of stars can be split; a detection cut short by the refinement budget is not flagged; there is no ROI, mask (`createStreakMask`), reclassification of a kept result, satellite or solar-system prediction, meteor radiant, tracking quality, or classification across frames.

## Annotation Contract

- One service, `AnnotationService` (`api/src/annotations.ts`, analysis jobs of the Shared Image Contracts with routes `/api/images/:id/annotations` and `/api/annotations` and event `annotations:updated`), identifies what an image document shows from its WCS, never from its pixels (manual runs only). The WCS is the solution of the completed plate solving job of the image the request names (`plateSolveJobId`), else the valid WCS of its header (`PlateSolvingService.result`/`solutions`), resolved and frozen when the job is submitted; a named job unknown, no longer kept, of another image, or without a solution, an image without a usable WCS, or a WCS that cannot unproject the image is refused (422 `contextUnavailable`). Nothing is solved implicitly, and a local job queries no network.
- The field is the cone around the unprojected image center that holds 8 unprojected points along each side (`annotationField`), plus 1′ for proper motion. The embedded sky catalog is scanned in memory for it (`fieldObjects`, `fieldConstellations` in `api/src/sky/catalog.ts`, behind the narrow `annotationSky` of `api/src/sky/index.ts`; a few milliseconds for the whole catalog, so the catalog has no spatial index): the stars and deep-sky objects up to the request's `magnitudeLimit`, deep-sky objects of a classic visual catalog without a magnitude too, at most 2000 of each (brightest first, `truncated` when more), and the stick figures with a segment reaching the cone, whole, with the labels inside it. An object is kept when `tanProject` puts it on the image; a deep-sky object when its major axis may reach the image.
- Positions are astrometric on the ICRS (J2000) equator of a plate solution, never apparent of date: deep-sky objects keep their catalog position, and stars are moved linearly by their proper motion to the observation instant. A local job has no solar-system body: the Sun, the Moon, the planets, and their satellites come from the solar-system search with the asteroids and comets.
- The observation context (`observationContext`) always comes from the image, for both kinds; a request carries no instant or observer. The instant is the middle of the exposure from the raw DATE-OBS of the header, else from its DATE-END when the exposure (EXPTIME or EXPOSURE) is known; FITS dates are UTC, and a date resolved to more than a second only is `approximate`. Without one, a local job moves the stars to the current time (origin `now`, with a warning) and a solar-system search is refused; a camera frame's command instant is not a fallback. The site is that of the header (OBSGEO-X/Y/Z, OBSGEO-B/L/H, or SITELAT/SITELONG/SITEELEV, read by the toolkit's `readFitsTime`), else the Earth's center.
- One local annotation runs at a time (provider `local`). An equivalent request (image, kind, WCS job, and the magnitude limit of a local job or the groups of a solar-system search) is answered with the job still waiting or running (202), or with its kept result.
- A result carries the WCS it was computed with (`solution`), so a client projects the objects with the same one, the field, the observation context, and each object's stable id (`catalog:<id>`, `body:<code>`, `satellite:<number or name>`, `skybot:<number or name>`), its `SkyObjectRef`, layer, names, magnitude, and size and position angle when the catalog has them; never pixels, screen positions, or SVG.
- Images → Annotation (`web/src/images/AnnotationPanel.tsx`, mirrored by `web/src/annotations/annotations.ts` in `SERVER_STORES`) sends the plate solution shown when it is a completed job of the image, else annotates with the header's WCS; Run Analysis is disabled without either and holds the frame a camera tab shows. The overlay (`web/src/annotations/AnnotationOverlay.tsx`) is drawn only over the image its result annotated and only while the solution shown is the one it was computed with (`annotationMatchesWcs`). Its geometry (`web/src/annotations/geometry.ts`) is computed once per result through `pointAt`: the outline of a deep-sky object is an ellipse of its catalog axes and position angle sampled on the sphere and then projected (a dashed circle of the major axis without an orientation or minor axis, a marker below 10 screen pixels), constellation figures are sampled along great circles, broken where the projection fails or jumps, and clipped to the image, and a body's predicted motion during the exposure is a dashed segment. Zoom and pan only place the labels again: a greedy, deterministic pass by priority (bodies, deep-sky objects, stars, then magnitude; the selected object first) over a spatial grid, within a budget per density (15, 40, 150), never over a marker. Groups (deep-sky objects, stars, the Sun, Moon, and planets, satellites, asteroids and comets, constellation lines and names), labels, density, outlines, motion, the magnitude limit (a lower one filters at once, a higher one applies to the next run), the groups the next solar-system search lists, and the selected object (by result job and object id) belong to the document view (`ImageAnnotationState`, started from the profile's `imageAnnotation`, see Persistence and State), so nothing of the overlay runs an analysis; a click within 8 screen pixels of a marker, or inside the extent of an object, and a row of the list select the same object, and the selection of a camera tab is cleared when another frame is committed. Open in Sky selects the object's `SkyObjectRef` in the Object Inspector and opens the Sky workspace without changing its time.
- A solar-system search (`kind: solarSystemBodies`, Find) is a separate job of the same routes and events, run by its own provider (`skybot`, one at a time) so it never waits for nor delays a local annotation. It asks the IMCCE SkyBoT cone search (`coneSearch` of `core/src/adapters/orbits/skybot.ts`) for the solar-system objects of the same cone at the observation instant, always from the geocenter (code 500): SkyBoT refuses a geographic observer on every interface despite its documentation. The request's `bodies` choose the groups listed, every one by default: `solarSystem` (the Sun, the Moon, and the planets, SkyBoT classes Star and Planet and the satellite Moon, which take the `SkyObjectRef` and the id of their body in the sky API, so they open in the Sky workspace), `satellite` (the other natural satellites), and `minorBody` (asteroids, dwarf planets, and comets); SkyBoT's object filter asks only for the classes needed, and the Sun, the planets, and the satellites, which it lists together, are sorted afterwards. When the header locates the image, every position, the motion ends included, is moved by the parallax of that site at its own instant (`geocentricToTopocentric` of `core/src/astronomy/coordinates/astrometry.ts`) from SkyBoT's geocentric distance; without a site, or for a body without a distance, it stays geocentric, with a warning. The search runs within 20 s (`timeout`; a failure of the service is `providerFailed`; cancelling aborts the request). SkyBoT fails a whole answer when it cannot compute an ephemeris of a planet or a satellite of the field, so a failed search that asks for both planets and minor bodies is repeated once for the asteroids and comets alone, within the same 20 s, and completes with a warning that the planets and their satellites are missing; a search of those groups alone fails with the message of the service. No other retry, fallback to another service, or background search exists. It is refused at submit (422 `contextUnavailable`) without an observation date, outside SkyBoT's epochs (1889 to 2060), for a cone beyond its 10° radius, or with no group. Kept objects are those on the image, at most 1000, brightest first, with SkyBoT's J2000 position, magnitude, position uncertainty, and motion during the exposure from its rates; a satellite or minor body has no `SkyObjectRef`: SkyBoT gives no SPK-ID. The magnitude limit never filters them. Every position says where it is seen from (`center` of the object and of the observation: `geocentricCorrected` computed for the geocenter and moved by the parallax of the site, `geocentric`), so a fallback is never shown as a topocentric prediction, and the observation carries the `site` used (latitude, longitude, elevation, and the keywords it came from). `GET /api/images/:id/annotations/context` gives the observation date and site of an image header (`AnnotationImageContext`) before any run. In the panel, Source & Status shows that date (UTC, with its keyword, or the current time without one) and the site with its keywords, the Solar System Bodies section the groups to list and how the positions will be seen; Find is disabled for an image without an observation date or with no group chosen. Its objects are listed and drawn with the local ones (the Sun, Moon, and planets as a diamond, a satellite as a smaller one, an asteroid or comet as a cross, the uncertainty as a dashed circle once larger than the marker); a satellite does not open in the Sky workspace, and Find in SBDB and Open in Sky looks the selected minor body up by its number or designation (`/api/sky/minorbodies/search`) and opens it only when SBDB knows exactly one body; a match is never guessed from a position.
- Limitations: the catalog is not complete to any magnitude, and the stars are those of the embedded catalog only; solar-system bodies need the network and SkyBoT, and their positions are predictions, not detections; the planets of a field SkyBoT cannot compute are not listed by any other source; no artificial satellites or nebula outlines; without a site in the header the Moon may be up to a degree off; the outline of a deep-sky object is the ellipse of its catalog axes, a dashed circle without an orientation; Open in Sky shows the object at the time of the Sky workspace, not of the image; there is no annotated image export (the overlay is drawn by the viewer only, and Save As writes the scientific pixels unchanged).

## Plate Solving Contract

- One service, `PlateSolvingService` (`api/src/platesolving.ts`), solves an image document for every consumer: Images → Plate Solver, TPPA, mount pointing sessions, and Target Centering. Captures never solve their frames. Providers are ASTAP, Astrometry.net Local (`solve-field`), and Nova Astrometry.net, each resolved once per job from External Tools; a request never names an executable or a URL. A provider never falls back to another, so a local failure never uploads an image. The libastrometry FFI binding is not a provider (see External Tools Contract).
- Strategies: `assisted` searches around a position, `blind` over the whole sky. Hints (`api/src/platesolving.hints.ts`) come from the operator, then from the header pointing (RA/DEC, OBJCTRA/OBJCTDEC, RA_OBJ/DEC_OBJ, CRVAL1/CRVAL2) and the optics (XPIXSZ after binning over FOCALLEN); a position without a radius gets 10°.
